An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

#2939. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2937. The plays that have set the tone for the Bengals’ defensive turnaround this season

It's no exaggeration to say Dexter Lawrence's sack against Tennessee Titans quarterback Cam Ward, late in the fourth quarter of Week 8, saved the Bengals' season.

On a day when the Bengals gave up 36 points and 440 yards, both season-highs for opposing offenses against the Bengals, the defense stepped up when they needed to. Lawrence's sack knocked the Titans out of field goal range inside the final minute of regulation with the score tied 36-36, and the Bengals capitalized in overtime with Joe Burrow running in a nine-yard touchdown on the Bengals' drive and the defense forcing a subsequent Titans four-and-out to close out the game.

Since that possession punctuated by Lawrence's sack, the Bengals' defense has been stellar. Against the Atlanta Falcons in Madrid, where the Falcons scored 10 points on their first two drives, the Bengals kept the Falcons off the scoreboard on six of their final seven possessions. Three of those six drives ended in punts, one ended in an interception, and another ended on a missed field goal.

This past Sunday against the Pittsburgh Steelers, the Bengals forced three-and-outs on the Steelers' first three drives, forcing five punts on 10 total Steelers drives. Cincinnati held Pittsburgh to 222 total yards Sunday night, a season-low for the Bengals' defense and just seven weeks after allowing 411 yards to the Steelers in Pittsburgh.

Going into Week 11, the Bengals' defense ranks No. 10 in scoring and No. 19 in yards allowed per game. They're allowing just 21.4 points per game and have just one game in their four games since the bye week where they've allowed more than 20 points.

Have there been some frustrating moments this season? Absolutely. Week 3 at Pittsburgh, Week 8 against Tennessee, and the first half at Baltimore in Week 7 were all frustrating and shades of 2024 and 2025.

But there have been a lot of bright moments so far this season that have set the tone for the Bengals' defensive turnaround. Going all the way back to Week 1, where the Bengals forced and recovered four fumbles and sacked Tampa Bay Buccaneers quarterback Baker Mayfield four times, there have been plays and games that have set the stage for the revived Bengals' defense to completely flip the narrative this season both locally and nationally.

Let's take a look at those key plays and games that have set the stage for the Bengals' defensive turnaround.

With 6:29 left in the first quarter, the Bengals trailed the Buccaneers 3-0. Tampa Bay drove 56 yards in 11 plays for a field goal on their opening drive, and the Bengals answered with a three-and-out. It wasn't a great start, especially with Mayfield and the Buccaneers' offense going right down the field on their opening drive.

But on 3rd&9 from the Buccaneers' own 33-yard line on Tampa Bay's second drive, Myles Murphy and Barrett Carter got to Mayfield with Murphy jarring the ball loose. Demetrius Knight Jr. scooped it up and raced 27 yards for the Bengals' first touchdown of the season, sending The Jungle into a frenzy.

Think about all three of the Bengals' defensive players who were involved in the play. Particularly Carter and Knight, who were much maligned as rookies in 2025. But on just their second possession of this season, Carter got to Mayfield and caused a fumble that Knight recovered and ran back for a touchdown. That set the tone for both Carter and Knight that they were going to play much faster this year and much more in a flow state.

Also, Murphy, who faced criticism over a lack of production for being the Bengals' first-round pick in 2023, finished this play by forcing the fumble. For a player who the Bengals decided not to pick up his fifth-year option, that play showed that Murphy is a vital part of the Bengals' deep wave of pass rushers.

Knight's scoop-and-score was the first of four fumbles the Bengals would force and recover on the day, and it was also their first of four sacks on Mayfield. The Bengals currently have a +3 turnover differential and 26 sacks through nine games this season. Carter has 74 tackles through nine games, and he's on pace for 140 this season, which would easily pass his 106 from 2025. He also has five tackles for loss, compared to just one last year, and he has five passes defensed and two forced fumbles.

Knight has 71 tackles and is on pace for 134 this season, easily surpassing his 106 from a year ago. He also has four tackles for loss this season, already equaling his total as a rookie. Murphy has 2.5 sacks, two tackles for loss, six quarterback hits, and a forced fumble. All three of these guys are having really good seasons. It was worth believing in them.

This game started as an offensive struggle on both sides. Then, though, the Texans' offense started to find some rhythm. On just their previous drive prior to Lawrence's 4th&1 stop, the Texans drove into the red zone. But a botched snap on a 34-yard field goal attempt denied the Texans the game's first score.

On their next drive, though, the Texans made it to the red zone again. This time, they faced a 4th&1 from the Bengals' 18-yard line. There was 6:59 left in the second quarter, and the Bengals had managed just 35 yards on their first four drives. But this is why the Bengals traded for Dexter Lawrence. It wasn't just to spark the defense; it was to spark the entire team.

The Texans ran a handoff to David Montgomery, ironically, a player they traded for this past offseason. Lawrence shot through the middle and stuffed Montgomery for a one-yard loss. After not showing up on the stat sheet a lot in Week 1, but still making a viable impact, Lawrence's stop on this 4th&1 was his first big-play moment with the Bengals. It was that feeling of 'Oh, this is why the Bengals went and got him.'

The Bengals answered by driving 81 yards in seven plays with Ja'Marr Chase hauling in a sensational 32-yard touchdown to put the Bengals up 7-0, swinging all of the momentum over to their side and taking the life out of the Reliant Stadium crowd.

Lawrence finished the game with three tackles and a tackle for loss, as well as a quarterback hit. The Bengals' defense finished with four sacks and eight tackles for loss, holding the Texans to just 60 yards rushing. Montgomery finished with just 10 yards on six carries.

The Bengals currently rank 21st in allowing 123.9 rushing yards per game. However, that's down from ranking dead last in 2025, allowing 147.1 rushing yards per game. There is marked improvement through nine games for the Bengals' run-defense, and Lawrence's 4th&1 stop in Houston showed his value and the Bengals' improvements in stopping the run. Lawrence currently has five sacks and nine tackles for loss through nine games, with 28 tackles, eight quarterback hits, and a fumble recovery.

Every Bengals fan is going to remember the game in Baltimore for Joe Burrow and the Bengals' offense exploding for 35 unanswered points in the second half, erasing a 13-0 halftime deficit for a 35-13 season-changing win. However, there's a defensive play from midway through the third quarter that stands out as another catalyst in the Bengals' defensive turnaround. It's not even the stop on 4th&2 on the first play of the fourth quarter or Demetrius Knight Jr.'s interception as the play I'm talking about.

The Bengals had just scored a touchdown on their first possession of the second half, cutting the deficit to 13-7. Momentum was starting to shift. But after allowing 258 yards to the Ravens in the first half, including going 8-11 on third down in the first 30 minutes, the Bengals now needed to flip the script on defense. On 2nd&7, Derrick Henry took a handoff to the right side. Mind you, when Derrick Henry takes a handoff to the perimeter, good luck. Especially considering the Bengals' run defense had not been good on the perimeter up to this point in the season.

But on this play, Jordan Battle, who is this Bengals' defense's most polarizing player, met Henry, took away his center of gravity, and pile-drove him backward four yards. Cashius Howell sacked Ravens' quarterback Tyler Huntley on the next play to force a three-and-out. The Bengals took a 14-13 lead on their next possession, and they never looked back from there.

Battle has clearly had his ups and downs this season. But his highs are incredible. Battle leads the Bengals with three interceptions and has eight passes defensed. He also has a forced fumble and 58 tackles this season.

Lawrence's crucial sack the next week against Tennessee proved that this defense is different. That game was wild and weird. The Bengals fell behind 13-0, then scored 21 unanswered points to lead 21-13. I thought the Bengals were in business at that point. They had weathered a slow start to take an eight-point lead. But the NFL is wild and weird sometimes.

Suddenly, this game became a shootout. The Bengals prevailed 43-36 in overtime. And even on a day when the Bengals' defense gave up a lot of points and a lot of yards, they made more than enough plays to win the game. It was a complete 180 from 2024 and 2025, when the Bengals were in shootout games. The Bengals of the last four years, the four years since their Super Bowl run in 2021, don't win the wild and weird games. The 2026 Bengals win these games.

Something that may get overlooked in this game is that the Titans only got three points off Joe Burrow's three interceptions. Those three points came off his first interception, when the Titans took a 13-0 lead late in the first quarter. After Burrow's second interception, with the Bengals leading 14-13 early in the third quarter, the Bengals' pass rush finally got to Cam Ward for the first time on the day.

Boye Mafe met him for a strip-sack, which Dexter Lawrence recovered and rumbled to the 17-yard line to set the Bengals up in the red zone. On the Bengals' first play of their ensuing drive, Burrow hit Tee Higgins for a 17-yard touchdown. Disaster turned into extending the Bengals' lead. Both sides picked each other up and complemented each other. Beautiful football. Mafe currently has 6.5 sacks and two forced fumbles, along with 12 quarterback hits. He's worth every dollar the Bengals paid him in free agency.

Burrow's third interception, where he underthrew Ja'Marr Chase, wasn't good. It caused tensions to rise on the Bengals' sideline. But two plays into the Titans' ensuing drive, with the score tied 21-21, Demetrius Knight Jr. shot into the backfield to tackle Titans running back Tony Pollard in the end zone for a safety. It gave the Bengals a 23-21 lead. Two points in that game were crucial, and it was the second time in the third quarter the Bengals' defense picked up the offense after a turnover.

This is what Bengals fans clamored for in 2024 and 2025. They begged for a defense like the one the Bengals have in 2026. Not just an average defense, but a defense that can change games. That's what this Bengals' defense is doing through nine games. Monday night, they'll head into Washington looking to avenge a loss to the Commanders from two years ago on Monday Night Football in Cincinnati. That game was the first sign that season the Bengals' defense may not be good. Two years later, the Bengals' defense is in a much better place. They've turned the corner. Now, they're ready to rev their engine alongside the Bengals' offense, which could potentially take this team to Los Angeles in February.

#2932. Kings hope Keegan Murray's late-game heroics vs. Celtics can get him on track

Kings hope Keegan Murray's late-game heroics vs. Celtics can get him on track originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SACRAMENTO — For a night, Keegan Murray finally has something to build off of.

The Kings forward's early season has been marred by overcoming an ankle injury stemming back to last year's frustrating 22-win finish. The one-time sharp shooter came into Saturday's game hitting a paltry 25 percent of his threes.

And after signing a five-year, $140 million contract last fall, Murray has been tabbed either a building block for the Kings or an expensive player for another team to acquire. Both of which raise expectations and add pressure to the shoulders of the quiet 26-year-old who should be entering his NBA prime.

All of which made Murray's primal scream after forcing a Payton Pritchard airball as time expired Saturday night notable.

Murray clamped the Boston Celtics point guard as he tried to make a step-back game winner. It came after Murray leaped over his former teammate Devin Carter to secure an offensive rebound and game-winning put back with 5.0 seconds remaining, giving Sacramento the ugly 97-96 victory, its second win of the season and first at Golden 1 Center.

It ended a miserable nine-game home winless streak to begin the year, the longest run in franchise history. It moved the Kings record to 2-14.

But more importantly for Murray, it might serve as rare signature moment. The Kings desperately need Murray to look like the player they envisioned him becoming if they're going to trend back towards respectability.

His 3-point shooting has declined in every season since his standout first year when he set a then-NBA rookie record with 206 made 3-pointers while shooting 41 percent from deep. He hasn't shot above 34 percent from 3-point range since 2024-25, and shot 28 percent from distance last season while playing just 23 games due to injury.

He started the year missing the first 15 games after suffering a torn ulnar collateral ligament in his left thumb. Then he suffered multiple ankle injures that led to two prolonged absences, including the final 22 games of the season. Murray said he had a setback with the ankle during the offseason and already has missed seven games this season because of it.

Saturday marked his seventh straight game played, his longest streak since last December.

"To see him go through all the things that he's gone through and to come out on the other side of that with some success is really a shining moment for him and for us, to be quite frank, because we all love him and want him to succeed," said embattled Kings coach Doug Christie.

Murray finished the game with 15 points and a season-high 12 rebounds (his previous high was seven he reached three times).

His defense also proved helpful while Celtics star Paul George shot just 6 of 21 and Pritchard made 4 of 17. George had 19 points while Pritchard finished with just nine. They shot just 34 percent as a team.

Boston was without star Jayson Tatum, who was given the night off to rest while the Celtics were playing the first night of a back to back. Guard Derrick White was out with a shoulder injury. They travel to San Francisco to play the Golden State Warriors on Sunday.

Darius Acuff Jr. led the Kings with 21 points on an inefficient 7-of-23 shooting. And while building a foundation around Acuff is imperative for Sacramento over the next few seasons, Murray's future is a significant variable. It's unclear whether Murray will stick around or be moved as part of the team's ongoing rebuilding project if the right trade comes along.

"He's stayed himself throughout the whole way," Acuff said of Murray. "He never changed. He was always bought in, never had bad energy. Brought energy every day no matter what the circumstances."

Murray, of course, speaks in monotone and is known for keeping to himself. He wasn't made available to speak to reporters after the game despite being one of the biggest stories of the night while the Kings earned a much needed win.

Shying from the spotlight is entirely unlike him. But that's the push and pull with having Murray on your team. His level of aggression has been a point of emphasis throughout his time in the league after the Kings selected him No. 4 overall in the 2022 NBA Draft.

He often has faded into the background, but the Kings are now paying him to be a game changer, and Saturday was the best example of the young season.

"It's never his fault," Zach LaVine said of Murray. "He's had to deal with a lot of stuff, coming back (from injury), playing with minutes restrictions, trying to get his groove back, get hurt again, that's tough.

"I've been injured in that situation. You don't feel like yourself sometimes. It takes time. … But he sticks with it. I see him every morning lifting, running, shooting. He's very consistent with his work, the stuff that nobody else sees, and he continues to do it regardless of what he gets paid."

Download and follow The Deuce & Mo Podcast

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released results from a survey of senior financial officers at banks about their strategies and practices for managing reserve balances. The Senior Financial Officer Survey is used by the Board to obtain information about banks' reserve balance management strategies and practices, their expectations around the size of their balance sheets and deposit balances their activity in money markets, and their views on other matters, such as operations on weekends and holidays.

The survey was conducted in collaboration with the Federal Reserve Bank of New York between September 18, 2026, and September 28, 2026, and includes responses from banks that held around three-fourths of total banking system reserve balances at the time of the survey.

#2926. Federal Reserve announces College Fed Challenge winners

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The University of California, Los Angeles won the 23rd Annual National College Fed Challenge.

The University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) won the 23rd annual national College Fed Challenge on Friday, a team competition that encourages undergraduate students to learn about the U.S. economy, monetary policymaking, and the role of the Federal Reserve System. Teams analyze economic and financial conditions and formulate a monetary policy recommendation, modeling the Federal Open Market Committee. The team, from Los Angeles, California, represented the San Francisco District and included Aarushi Maheshwari, Annika Chan, Atul Prasanth, Caitlyn Cox, Finn Karish, Eli Brooks (alternate), Kennedy Yang (alternate), Manasi Vartak (alternate), Ronojoy Borpujari (alternate), and Sahil Sudani (alternate). The team's advisers were Chris Surro and Ali Haider Ismail.

"I am very happy to congratulate the students who competed in this year's College Fed Challenge, especially the winners and finalists," said Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh. "They took on the same big questions we policymakers face. I'm impressed by their talent, their rigor, and their eagerness to think hard about monetary policy and the economy."

This year's College Fed Challenge included 141 schools from 34 states across the nation, the most in the competition's history. Teams submitted video presentations or participated in local competitions last month. Twenty-one semi-finalist teams then participated in question-and-answer sessions held earlier this month, and seven finalists were invited to the Federal Reserve Board for the national finals. The other national finalists were the University of Chicago in second place and the University of Maryland, College Park in third place. Teams with honorable mentions include Cleveland State University, Harvard College, Pennsylvania State University, and St. Lawrence University.

Teams were evaluated on their economic analysis, responses to the judges' questions, teamwork, and presentations.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

20th Street and Constitution Avenue N.W., Washington, DC 20551

#2923. Celtics will be without 3 key players against Kings

SACRAMENTO — The Celtics will be without Jayson Tatum, Derrick White, and Mitchell Robinson when they face the Sacramento Kings on Saturday night.

For the first time this season, Tatum is resting on the first night of a back-to-back; the Celtics play the Warriors on Sunday at 8:30pm ET. White is out with a left AC joint contusion (the acromioclavicular joint connects the collarbone to the top of the shoulder blade), while Robinson is out with illness.

Tatum played in both nights of the Celtics' first three back-to-backs of the season, and said in Charlotte that it would be something the time would play by ear.

"It's my 10th year, so I understand the balance of wanting to play every game and rest and back-to-backs, three in four nights, five in seven," Tatum said. "It's always a conversation. We talk about it. We have a plan. I don't really like sitting out on away games, but we just kind of figure it out as we go."

Tatum said he did feel good in the back-to-backs that he played in.

"I used to always play back-to-backs, and obviously health is most important, and how I'm feeling physically," Tatum said. "But it was an opportunity for me to test myself out to see how I felt playing the back-to-backs, traveling, getting in at a certain time. See how I felt after that, and I kind of just really wanted to go through that for myself, and I felt really good."

White will miss his first game of the season, while Robinson will miss his second (he missed one earlier this season due to rest).

With three rotation players out, it's likely that backups like Sam Hauser, Baylor Scheierman, Hugo Gonzalez, Luka Garza, and Chris Cenac Jr. will alll see increased minutes.

The Kings, who are 1-14 on the season, will be without Ben Simmons, who is out for the season with a right Achilles tendon rupture. Maxime Raynaud is out with a right patella subluxation, and Precious Achiuwa is questionable with a left low leg contusion.

#2919. Trump vows to stop U.S. companies from building data centers abroad, threatens 'tremendous tax'

President Donald Trump on Friday said U.S. companies will be banned from building data centers in other countries and will face a "tremendous tax" penalty if they do so.

"We're going to fight very hard for the data centers," Trump said at a White House press conference before announcing the new policy — which apparently came as news to his own staff.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story," Trump said. "I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now they're here."

Trump's latest push for more data-center construction in the U.S. came despite Americans repeatedly stating — and showing with their votes — that they dislike the structures.

CNBC's All-America Economic Survey found that 64% of registered voters opposed an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% of respondents said they would support one.

If Trump follows through on his threat to clamp down on overseas data center construction, he will have to contend with the fact that numerous U.S. tech giants — including Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Meta and Oracle — have already built them in other countries.

International expansion represents a growth opportunity for the top cloud infrastructure providers, which want to serve clients from data centers that are nearby, sometimes to meet regulatory requirements.

Trump said Friday, however, that if companies want to continue building the centers outside the U.S., "they can, but we're going to put a tremendous tax on them."

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy, and they have everything. They're tremendous assets," Trump said.

Representatives at cloud providers Amazon, Google, Microsoft and Oracle did not immediately respond to requests for comment.

Trump's remarks came a day after Trump met with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin in China for a trilateral summit that appeared to produce few concrete deliverables on an array of pressing global issues.

Xi "congratulated me on how well we're doing" in artificial intelligence, Trump said, using his preferred term "super intelligence," which he said the Chinese president also likes better.

— CNBC's Jordan Novet and Annie Palmer contributed reporting.

#2917. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2913. AI is changing software engineering — and some developers miss the struggle

When Gemma Rodgers began her computer science degree in 2002, software engineering was seen as a future-proof and lucrative career. It appealed to people who enjoyed getting into the weeds of code, tracking down bugs and building programs from scratch.

It's an industry that has always moved fast, Rodgers, a software developer based in the U.K., told CNBC. But the arrival of artificial intelligence has accelerated the pace, with AI tools now part of the daily work of every software engineer she knows. In a 2026 Stack Overflow survey, 83% of software developer respondents said they use at least one AI tool.

As AI coding tools help engineers write, debug and test code faster than ever, some are mourning the job they used to have. Rodgers has always loved the sense of accomplishment that comes from piecing together different parts of code to find a solution, particularly when she's spent days, or even weeks, trying to fix a stubborn bug.

"The sense of joy and fun that I used to experience by having a challenge and then having to work that out, it's been removed somewhat," she said. "Now you can sometimes do it in a few prompts, and it's great — but it's a breadcrumb of joy, not a full meal of joy."

Rodgers is not alone in feeling nostalgic about the not-so-old days. Software engineers have taken to social media to describe missing their old way of working, when they could get into a flow state with code for hours at a time.

Still, AI tools can be useful and have lowered the barrier to entry significantly. "It's becoming an industry where a lot of people have been able to thrive who wouldn't necessarily have been able to thrive 10 years ago, five years ago, even three years ago," Rodgers said.

It's also important that the core skills engineers have traditionally learned on the job are not lost, she said. AI tools and internet services can go down unexpectedly, and engineers still need to know how to investigate problems and work through solutions themselves.

"The sorts of skills that we were taught years ago are kind of being lost because [engineers are] having to just churn work out," Rodgers added.

The job itself is changing, too. James Broadhead, chief technology officer of AI decision-making company Taktile, told CNBC that engineers who might previously have spent three weeks building a prototype can now generate 10 in an afternoon. The hardest part of that, he said, is "having the judgment to decide which of those are any good."

"The [AI] agents are spitting out so much code that [engineers] haven't read it all anymore," Broadhead said. "It used to be you'd build up a sense of comfort and awareness of how the whole code base was structured, but now the whole thing changes all the time."

Joshua Wöhle, CEO of London-based AI training company Mindstone, told CNBC he sees a growing divide between workers who know how to use AI effectively and those who don't.

The industry used to talk about "10X engineers," he said, who were dramatically more productive than their peers. Now, AI is widening that gap even further to produce "100X engineers."

Wöhle believes companies will increasingly have to decide whether to help employees catch up with AI or prioritize those who can get the most out of it. But he also understands why some engineers might struggle to adapt.

The people who once excelled at the job were often those who loved writing and working through code, he said, and those are not necessarily the current top performers.

"That's the grief that is very, very real," Wöhle said. "The thing you love doing is no longer the thing the company should be paying for, to a degree, because it's no longer adding the value to the company that it used to."

But not every engineer sees the move away from hands-on coding as a bad thing. Chris Sees, a software engineer of more than 20 years and CEO of London-based virtual office company Hoxton Mix, told CNBC that AI gives developers more time to tackle interesting problems. He views AI as a capable junior engineer that still needs supervision.

"The important thing is knowing what to delegate and when human judgement is needed," Sees said. "We'd be silly to think AI gets everything right, and I know I get a lot of satisfaction out of proving and spotting where it has gone wrong."

Even so, Sees worries about what happens to the next generation of engineers if AI takes over the routine coding and debugging through which they traditionally learned. Those tasks are "where you learn lessons and build scar tissue," he said, and companies need to make sure AI becomes part of the learning process, rather than a shortcut.

For Rodgers, what matters is having control over how she uses the technology. As a self-employed developer, she can use AI to automate the parts of a project she finds tedious, while keeping the more interesting problem-solving for herself.

She believes there's something important about working through a difficult problem rather than immediately outsourcing it to technology. Friction and discomfort, she said, are vital parts of the human experience.

"As humans, we need to feel accomplished in something and part of accomplishment requires challenge," she said. "Unless we are able to work that through in so many different aspects of life, then you never get the full force of the good feeling on the other side."

#2909. Europe's cyber weak spot is the everyday businesses nobody thought to protect

Cyberattacks on refrigerators in French supermarkets in October spurred Swedish chain Coop to act. But it wasn't fast enough.

Attackers hit refrigeration systems in seven of its stores in early November. The affected stores had been scheduled for security upgrades just two days later, a company spokesperson told CNBC.

Mundane but essential equipment, used by everyday businesses, has become a favorite target of cyber saboteurs in recent weeks.

Experts are warning that a combination of known vulnerabilities, basic security failures and ambiguity over whether suppliers or their clients are responsible are making things worse, as AI makes cyber threats faster and more sophisticated.

Guy Segal, CEO of cybersecurity firm Sygnia, told CNBC that his company handled 285% more incidents in October than a year earlier, with demand forcing it to turn away some businesses seeking emergency assistance.

"It's going to get worse before it's going to get better," Segal said.

The recent cyberattacks targeted refrigeration systems across hundreds of European supermarkets, remotely altering temperature settings and forcing retailers to discard spoiled food.

France has since accused a cyber unit with links to Iran's Revolutionary Guard Corps of the attacks, an allegation Tehran denies.

Fredrik Blix, a cybersecurity researcher at Stockholm University, told CNBC that thousands of industrial control systems remain accessible over the public internet, sometimes despite manufacturers' warnings.

Segal said that basic cyber protection measures such as multi-factor authentication and basic network segmentation are more important than ever.

He said risks to major civic infrastructure like the electrical grid were front of mind and so "usually pretty well protected."

"There are always those civil supply chain components that are way, way less protected. Could be supermarkets, could be gas stations, could be… the screens on the Tube," Segal said, referring to London's underground rail system.

The supermarket attacks showed "this is the new normal, and definitely it's a wake-up call for every company out there," Segal added.

Segal said that inexpensive AI tools mean technical expertise and resources are no longer needed to launch attacks. He described one recent incident in which clients suffered breaches within six hours of a vulnerability becoming known, as perpetrators used AI to act faster — a process that might have taken weeks a year earlier.

Segal, whose firm helps companies respond to breaches and strengthen their defenses, warned that businesses without prearranged incident-response support could struggle to secure emergency assistance, as overwhelmed cybersecurity firms prioritize existing clients.

In one case, he said, Sygnia had to turn down a company because no response teams were available. Segal added that the company in question had already been turned down by ten other providers.

Jacob Silverman, CEO of risk advisory firm Kroll, told CNBC earlier this month that the company was operating at full capacity in its incident-response business amid what he described as "the most rapid uptick in threat activity that we've ever seen."

Segal argued that retailers should treat cybersecurity risks with the same seriousness as fire safety and food hygiene, including when outsourcing maintenance.

When Ahold Delhaize stores in Belgium and Luxembourg were attacked on Nov. 2, it exposed how different independent store operators had responded to the warnings following the earlier attacks in France.

Retailers had been warned about the vulnerabilities following earlier attacks in France, and those that implemented the recommended protections were spared, CEO Frans Muller told investors in an earnings call earlier this month, but smaller operators with fewer technical resources had struggled to make the necessary changes.

Coop, whose stores are owned by regional consumer cooperatives or operated by franchises, told CNBC that public attention on the recent attacks was the main driver of security improvements. It said the vulnerability at the affected stores had since been addressed.

The financial consequences of cyberattacks can be substantial. Ahold Delhaize told investors in November that a separate October cyberattack on its online operations and supply chain would reduce fourth-quarter sales by 200 million euros ($219 million).

Danish shipping giant Maersk, which suffered an estimated $250 million to $300 million in losses from the 2017 NotPetya cyberattack, has spent nearly a decade strengthening its defenses.

CEO Vincent Clerc told CNBC earlier this month that the experience had prompted significant investment in security capabilities, but warned that AI was enabling attackers to become more effective.

"Everybody is in a race" to boost defenses, he said.

Research by cybersecurity firm Censys found that the number of internet-exposed refrigeration controllers made by Danfoss and Copeland exposed to cyber hacking fell 53% globally between Oct. 9 and Nov. 5.

However, more than 3,100 remained exposed, and Censys said it could not determine whether the decline reflected permanent security improvements or equipment simply being taken offline.

A Copeland spokesperson confirmed that some of its refrigeration systems were affected by the vulnerability and said it had contacted customers to reinforce cybersecurity guidance, including keeping devices off the public internet and updating software.

Danfoss didn't respond to CNBC requests for comment.

In the U.K., a covert cybersecurity operation has been underway to protect tens of thousands of refrigeration systems at supermarkets, warehouses, restaurants and other businesses, the BBC reported on Friday. After researchers discovered a major vulnerability in late October, cybersecurity specialists, IT teams and the U.K.'s National Cyber Security Centre worked to alert dozens of companies and secure their systems before hackers could exploit the flaw.

The EU's Cyber Resilience Act will also impose stricter security requirements on manufacturers of connected products from December 2027.

But the legislation does not require all existing equipment to be made compliant with the new standards.

Blix told CNBC on Nov. 6 that European authorities should take a more proactive approach to reducing vulnerabilities by identifying exposed industrial control systems and contacting their operators before attacks occur.

"What worries me is that we don't really do anything," he said, adding that "we are shuffling a lot of papers" and "doing a lot of risk assessments" but failing to take meaningful action.

#2899. A massive bearish trade just happened in the chip stocks

So much for buying the dip.As the S&P 500 entered correction territory and the Nasdaq-100 flirted with bear-market territory, down 19% from highs as of midday Friday, the single biggest options trade of the day was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH) has a long way more to fall.Around 12:30 E.T., someone spent $93 million buying 20,000 380-strike puts expiring Sep. 17, 2027, according to trading data reviewed by SpotGamma and Volland, options-analytics platforms that both show the trade as a likely opening position, with volume exceeding existing open interest in the contract. The trade bets that the index will fall below $380, a nearly 8% decline from its current level. The SMH is off 40% from its recent all-time highs.

"That trade stands out big-time," wrote Jason DeLorenzo, founder and owner of Volland. "It is either a hedge or speculative, but either way it isn't a vote of confidence."The puts were bought for $46.50 a piece, an arguably expensive choice for a downside bet with volatility in the ETF trading at 70.

"You're paying a metric ton of Vega for that thing," added SpotGamma's Brent Kochuba, referring to the contract's sensitivity to changes in volatility of the underlying chip ETF. "Maybe they were a forced buyer, or maybe they're actually selling this, which can still be the case even though the price shows it happening closer to the ask."

The semiconductor index headed for a 9% drop this week alone.

Keys to the GameDefense – Defense is always the key to winning every game. The Kings have a defensive rating of 120.4(30th). The Celtics defensive rating is 106.0, 2nd in the league. By doing so, they limit the second chance points and fast breaks for the Kings and also give themselves extra possessions. They had 8 consecutive turnovers at one point of the 3rd quarter. The Celtics have to focus on taking better care of the ball.

Against the Nuggets, the Celtics shot just 25% on threes and went cold in the 2nd half. If the Celtics aren't hitting their 3s, they have to go to Plan B and take the ball to the basket or shoot from midrange. If the 3s are falling, great!! If not, go to Plan B.

Fatigue could come into play for them. Also they need to ignore the distractions of travel, an unfamiliar arena and a hostile crowd and concentrate on playing together, playing hard, and playing the right way.

Players Out – With Jayson Tatum, Derrick White and Mitchell Robinson all out due to the back to back games, other Celtics are going to need to step up and pick up the slack left by the 3 players being out. They will need to pick up both defensive and offensive slack along with more effort on the boards. With 2 key starters out, the reserves are going to need to play harder and smarter. Even though the Kings have only won 1 game so far, the Celtics should not underestimate them and they need to play their best, even with 3 players out.

Officiating – Officiating can always be an x-factor. Every crew will call the game differently. Some will call every little thing and the game will have no flow. Others will let a lot go and let them play. Some crews favor the home team while others call it evenly or have a personal agenda. In the second half against the Nuggets, the Nuggets shot 22 free throws to just 3 for the Celtics. The Celtics need to adjust to how the game is called and not let the officials mess with their focus.

#2892. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2891. Why Anthropic's blockbuster IPO couldn't escape the stock market correction

No one can say the Anthropic initial public offering wasn't a success for the company. Deal size and pricing were signs of what IPO experts say was insatiable demand for "ANT" shares. But what came within a day is something that investors better get used to: anxiety in the stock market about valuations being assigned to artificial intelligence revenue and business models that should keep Anthropic shares volatile in the period ahead.

The Anthropic IPO reinforces those fears rather than running counter to them, says Avery Marquez, director of investment strategies at Renaissance Capital, which researches IPOs and manages the Renaissance IPO ETF (IPO).

"The volatility in the AI trade we've been seeing for the past few months stems from valuations and whether we are in a bubble, and that's been heightened and highlighted by the Anthropic IPO," Marquez said on "ETF Edge" on Friday.

And in the case of Anthropic, which finished trading Friday down 9%, the valuation anxiety becomes "so much more prevalent," she added, because it is a new stock.

It is not just any new stock, but one that has Wall Street adding downside risks to research notes likely never seen before, and which may be the greatest understatement (or most subtle equities research joke) in the history of earnings analysis. "Anthropic could develop AI systems which become fully autonomous and wipe out humanity which may lead to a serious decline in earnings," Seaport Research Partners analyst wrote Jay Goldberg wrote on Nov. 18.

"It was challenging company to value ... and it's very richly valued," Marquez said. "We're seeing something more reasonable and investors grappling now with where should this company actually be valued," she added.

The fact that Anthropic pulled off the deal in this market and was able to command the price it did was an achievement, according to Yuri Khodjamirian, chief investment officer at Tema ETFs, which offers multiple funds that invest in pre-IPO shares of companies and held Anthropic in its Tema Photonics & Optical ETF (LAZR) well before IPO day. "We have to remember they pulled off a very large IPO in the middle of a very severe market with the volatility in the tech sector specifically," Khodjamirian said on "ETF Edge."

Khodjamirian says the investor demand for AI remains, but amid a reassessment of returns. And he says an equally hard task has now begun for Anthropic. Investors are unwinding positions in everything from tech stocks to gold and bitcoin, and repositioning for 2027 based on their new outlooks. "We are in a bit of vacuum," he said.

"From here on the market is trying trying to figure out what direction to go in," Khodjamirian said, whether that is based on Fed policy, geopolitics, the midterm elections results, or the AI trade. "In many cases, the chief investment officers I speak to, and the clients, say 'I'm just gonna take some risk off and wait and see until I get more info.' And with the Anthropic, the revenue picture and how the AI adoption curve progresses will be a vital stat to watch," he added.

Plenty of value has already been wiped out in tech, according to Khodjamirian, but investors need new signals to buy. That doesn't just go for Anthropic but for much of the market's tech leadership. "One of the things most difficult for investors to understand right now is some of the leadership in the market that has been taken by semiconductor companies," he said, adding that the peak was 22% of S&P 500 earnings. But these companies, like Nvidia and Micron, have a very different earnings profile from the rest of the market.

The VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH), which was down over 10% on Friday and around 15% for the week, was on pace for its worst week since September 2001, according to CNBC data on Friday afternoon. It also lost 15% in one week of March 2020. The tech sector of the S&P 500, tracked by the State Street Technology Select Sector SPDR ETF (XLK), turned in its worst weekly performance since the Covid crash.

Khodjamirian said while these stocks may look cheap on a P/E basis right now after the heavy selling, that requires faith in the earnings estimates, which he says will be more important as results are released for the next few years. "Right now it looks cheap, but will companies like Micron and Nvidia actually deliver?"

Nvidia shares fell 14% on Friday, adding to its worst stretch of weekly performance since 2008. What's more, the single biggest trade in the options market on Friday was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF has a long way more to fall.

And the same caution about the outlook goes for Anthropic, he said. "That's why we need to watch Anthropic's annual recurring revenue number, because it is a key part of puzzle," he said.

Marquez said there is always hype in names like SpaceX and Anthropic when they go public, and it is hard to know whether the pricing of the deal is based on fundamentals, "but we just need more time and lots price discovery," she said. "It is not going to happen overnight," she added.

As an IPO researcher, she says this "discernment" is a healthy thing for the markets. There are few cases to look to as comps for Anthropic, other than SpaceX, and before that, way back to Facebook's IPO in 2012. And neither example is one of quick recovery. SpaceX shares remain well below their first-day closing price more than five months after its IPO. Facebook (now Meta), meanwhile, "did not do well for the first year after going public and we see where it is now. It's just the nature of these disruptive tech companies that come to market," Marquez said. "They lend themselves to more volatility and a longer period of price discovery for investors."

All eyes will next be on Anthropic's rival OpenAI, which has suggested it will be ready to go public in 2027. Marquez said the reaction to the Anthropic deal may be a signal that it was "a good idea to wait, allowing themselves to build more quarters of better financials and improve fundamentals a bit before taking on a market that is really quite discerning."

Tema does not own shares of OpenAI in any of its ETFs. But, "I can't say it wont be," Khodjamirian said. "They exist out there in the secondary market, and we will look at it, but we haven't made any decision. It depends on the ETF fit and if the portfolio manager wants to invest. We are always looking out for these interesting businesses but never just for the sake of private exposure," he added.

Tema does own Databricks, Stripe, Kalshi and Polymarket across its funds. It also was an investor in SpaceX pre-IPO through its Tema Space Innovators ETF (NASA).

Khodjamirian said demand from public market investors to access these "generational businesses" will continue to be the case, despite the market volatility, and will more often come before IPO day. In fact, he pointed to the filing from the world's largest asset manager and one of the biggest ETF companies in the world, BlackRock, earlier this week. It wants to bring out a new kind of exchange-traded product that would bring the ETF and interval fund structures together as a way to increase exposure to private companies, with shares trading on an exchange while the fund makes monthly repurchase offers.

#2885. An early breakdown in the polar vortex is threatening already depleted gas stocks

An unprecedented early breakdown of the stratospheric polar vortex and a record-breaking El Niño event has put energy traders on alert this winter, with analysts warning the weather and climate phenomena could have an outsized impact on Europe.

Meteorologists are currently observing a record early breakdown of the polar vortex, a large-scale circulation of strong winds that typically keeps extremely cold air over the North Pole.

Changes in the strength and shape of the polar vortex can influence weather patterns much lower down in the atmosphere, potentially bringing in colder weather to parts of Europe and North America.

This year, however, the collapse of the stratospheric polar vortex, or SPV, is taking place at the same time as an unusually powerful El Niño, a climate event that can boost global temperatures and disrupt rainfall.

The collision of these two events raises the risk of a prolonged cold spell that could lift heating demand and force European countries to draw down already-low reserves faster. That would leave the continent more reliant on imported liquefied natural gas (LNG) at a time when supplies from the Middle East are disrupted.

To be sure, Europe's natural gas storage levels started the winter at their lowest level in more than a decade.

The International Energy Agency warned earlier this month that low European storage levels and limited storage options in several LNG-importing countries in Asia and South America could intensify the competition for cargoes and put upward pressure on prices during periods of high demand.

Simon Lee, a lecturer in atmospheric science at the U.K.'s University of St Andrews, said the combination of a sudden stratospheric warming (SSW) with an El Niño event is not necessarily unusual, but meteorologists had been expecting a polar vortex breakdown in February — not as early as November.

The early SPV disruption is an "extremely rare" event that could influence winter weather in northwest Europe and the eastern U.S., Lee said, increasing the chance of colder conditions although likely not to the extent that a polar vortex event caused parts of Niagara Falls to partially freeze in January 2014.

"Geopolitics and weather are the two key volatility drives for energy and now they seem to be happening at the same time — and that's always the biggest risk for markets," Florence Schmit, senior energy strategist at Rabobank, told CNBC.

For months, meteorologists expected an El Niño event would result in a milder winter in Europe and relatively muted heating demand, Schmit said, which was broadly seen as welcome news given ongoing concerns over the continent's tight regional supplies for gas and crude oil.

The prospect of a colder-than-expected winter due to an early breakdown of the polar vortex could be "incredibly difficult" for Europe to navigate, Schmit warned. She noted that the region's gas storages have seen "very, very strong withdrawals" over the last two weeks, "which is quite unusual for this time of year."

"If you continue to see the same storage withdrawals over the next couple of months, that means energy prices will spike again — and they won't spike because of geopolitics. This time it will be the weather driving that spike and that volatility," Schmit said.

European gas prices have soared to levels not seen since the 2022 energy crisis in recent weeks, notching 103.9 euros ($113.99) per megawatt hour in late October. European benchmark Dutch TTF futures for December delivery stood at around 87.28 euros on Friday afternoon.

"We have never seen an early breakdown of the polar vortex and a super el Nino in the same winter so we have no historical precedent on how this will impact global gas demand," David Lewis, principal research analyst of gas and liquified natural gas at Wood Mackenzie, told CNBC by email.

El Niño is commonly associated with milder winters, while a SSW event typically leads to colder temperatures and higher snowfall, Lewis said, noting this could materialize in December and January within Europe.

"So far, the start of the European winter heating season has been quite cold but more importantly has seen low wind speeds across Northern Europe," Lewis said.

"The drawdown in stocks in the first half of November has been amongst the highest on record. With inventories starting the winter at their lowest level on record, any further deterioration in temperatures or wind speeds will have an outsized impact on European balances and lead to higher prices across Europe," he added.

#2884. Red Wings Defenseman Won’t Sit in a Box

The unique element Andreas Englund brings to the Detroit Red Wings' defense is that he plays the game with an edge.

Perhaps the most important quality that Englund brings to the Red Wings is that he doesn't go over that edge.

Ross Johnston and Andreas Englund drop the gloves 🥊 pic.twitter.com/orC53cSm0A

— Sportsnet (@Sportsnet) November 11, 2026

A willing and capable fighter, Englund brings a much-appreciated physical presence to the Detroit back end. His 51 hits lead the Red Wings rearguard and rank eighth overall among NHL defensemen. Englund's 13.19 hits per 60 minutes place him third in the league among defenders with at least 10 games played.

At the same time, Englund rarely crosses the line into malfeasance. Through 17 games this season, the 30-year-old Swedish rearguard has been assessed a solitary minor penalty.

"For how he plays and as much as he's tangled up with players in and around the net and along the boards, he doesn't go to the penalty box very often," Red Wings coach Todd McLellan said. "That's a good thing for us.

"Penalty kill is obviously not one of our strengths right now."

Players of Englund's ilk are often pigeonholed, their limitations viewed in a certain specific light. Englund, though, adamantly refuses to enable anyone to put him in a box.

"Obviously you don't want to be in a box if you don't have to," Englund said. "To be in [the lineup], and trying to limit my minors to a minimum.

"This is, I think, a key for my game. To not sit in a box."

It's an impressive feat for any defenseman to go without taking penalties. The nature of the position is to engage in combative physical battles with opponents. It can become second nature when losing those battles to grab the opponent, or give him a tug with your stick.

"Yeah, I mean, it sounds cliché and stuff, but it's like just moving your feet and making sure you don't get trapped with your stick in the wrong places," Englund said.

The Red Wings are Englund's seventh NHL stop. He was claimed on waivers from the Calgary Flames.

At the time, it seemed a puzzling move for a team that was deep on defense. Watching Englund play steadily, if unspectacularly, in Detroit's third pairing, it's evident that McLellan saw something in the player when he had him previously in Los Angeles.

— Detroit Red Wings (@DetroitRedWings) September 26, 2026

#2881. Zscaler stock jumps 18% on report that CrowdStrike in talks to buy security vendor

Zscaler shares surged 18% in extended trading on Friday after Bloomberg reported that CrowdStrike is in talks to buy the cybersecurity vendor. Shares of CrowdStrike shares fell more than 8%.

The talks between the two companies are ongoing, Bloomberg reported, citing sources familiar in the matter. CrowdStrike declined to comment on the report. Zscaler didn't immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

CrowdStrike has been one of the big cybersecurity winners in the artificial intelligence boom, with its stock about doubling in value this year, lifting its market cap to $235 billion. Zscaler, meanwhile, is down 13% this year, pushing its market cap down to about $32 billion.

Along with CrowdStrike, Palo Alto Networks has seen its business boom as businesses race to upgrade their networks in the AI era. Palo Alto closed Friday at a nearly $282 billion market cap.

Should a deal take place, it would be among the largest in the history of the cybersecurity industry. Google acquired Wiz last year for $32 billion. In 2024, Cisco paid about $28 billion for Splunk. Earlier this year, Palo Alto closed its purchase of CyberArk for $25 billion.

CrowdStrike's deals to this point have been on the smaller side.The company announced plans earlier this month to acquire agentic observability platform Fiddler AI and AI trust platform Dreadnode.

#2880. Lakers rookie makes most of biggest opportunity he’s gotten so far

Lakers coach JJ Redick has emphasized for weeks that every player on his roster is going to get an opportunity to play.

The nature of an 82-game regular season necessitates flexibility with playing rotations.

Cameron Carr of the Los Angeles Lakers drives to the basket. NBAE via Getty Images

During Friday's 106-104 home win over the Kings, rookie Cameron Carr got his most significant opportunity with the Lakers to date.

And his performance showed why he's deserving of more playing opportunities moving forward.

With Austin Reaves sidelined for the fourth consecutive game because of a bruised left heel, Carr was in the rotation off the bench, playing 11 minutes.

Unlike some of his previous playing time, a good amount of which has come when the game's results were already decided, the former Baylor Bear's minutes had a direct impact on the Lakers' chances of winning.

And the rookie made the playing time count, having five points on 2-for-5 shooting (1-for-4 on 3-pointers), a steal and a rebound.

"He looked great," Walker Kessler said. "He hit some big shots, played with physicality. He's got some long arms, I can tell you that. That layup he made — he's just got some long arms. He looked good tonight. I was happy. It was good to see."

Cameron Carr of the Los Angeles Lakers speaks to the media. NBAE via Getty Images

The stats don't matter as much as the comfort Carr showed when he was on the court.

He didn't hesitate to shoot the corner 3-pointer that momentarily gave the Lakers a two-point lead midway through the second quarter. And didn't shy away from contact on a layup later in the quarter.

Carr looked ready for the defensive physicality needed to hold his own on that end of the floor.

"That was huge," Quentin Grimes said. "I mean, as a rookie, you got to go out there and kind of earn minutes. Everybody kind of has to do that at one point some time in their career. And he got thrown in there, hit a big 3, hit a tough layup and had a couple good closeouts and got some stops. So, those are the things that earn trust from a coach when they throw you out there even more later in the season, for sure."

Redick has said Carr and second-year forward Adou Thiero's development over the next couple of years could raise the team's collective ceiling.

But before they can be ceiling-raisers, Carr and Thiero need to establish a floor for themselves.

Jaden Hardy #7 and Cameron Carr #43 of the Los Angeles Lakers smile during a game against the Sacramento Kings. NBAE via Getty Images

With Reaves expected to make his return to the floor next week, playing time won't be available as it has been with him sidelined.

But Carr showed, at least for one game, he's ready to contribute more when his number is called.

And it's on the Lakers to find more chances for him so he can develop into the ceiling-raiser they're looking for him to be.

#2879. Retirees face a looming deadline for required withdrawals — missing it could trigger a tax penalty

Every year, millions of Americans must take required withdrawals from retirement accounts by Dec. 31.

Amid the busy holiday season, it's easy to miss the year-end deadline for required minimum distributions — a mistake that can trigger a hefty IRS penalty, experts said.

"One of the most common RMD mistakes is waiting until the last minute," said certified financial planner Alex Canellopoulos, director of wealth at Vista Capital Partners in Portland, Oregon.

Most retirees must start RMDs from pretax accounts at age 73. The withdrawal is based on the previous year's account balances on Dec. 31, your age and an IRS "life expectancy factor."

The first annual RMD deadline is April 1 of the year after you turn 73, and future withdrawals are due by Dec. 31. If you wait until April 1 for the first one, you'll need two RMDs that year.

RMDs also apply to certain inherited IRAs if the original IRA owner reached RMD age before their death.

In 2023, more than 14.8 million taxpayers age 70 or older had a traditional individual retirement account, according to the latest IRS data. The average year-end market value of the accounts was about $330,000.

Deferring RMDs until the last minute raises the likelihood of mistakes, such as overlooking certain retirement accounts or making withdrawal calculation errors, Canellopoulos said.

Some 7% of Vanguard investors at RMD age missed their yearly withdrawal in 2025, up slightly from 6.7% in 2024, according to a Vanguard analysis of about 400,000 RMD-age retirees with IRA balances published in December 2025.

Among those investors, the average missed RMD was $10,500 in 2025, Vanguard told CNBC.

Investors who don't take RMDs by the annual deadline may owe an excise tax to the IRS, worth up to 25% of the amount they should have withdrawn. For example, the excise tax could be up to $2,625 for a $10,500 missed RMD.

If investors miss a required withdrawal, they could reduce the penalty to 10% if they "timely corrected" the RMD within two years and file Form 5329, according to the IRS.

In some cases, the IRS could waive the penalty entirely due to "reasonable error" if investors take "reasonable steps" to fix the shortfall, according to the agency. Investors can file Form 5329 with a letter of explanation to request the waiver.

"If you don't ask, you're not going to get it," said certified public accountant Tim Steffen, director of advanced planning at financial services firm Baird. "I've never heard of anybody being denied the waiver."

Investors who give to charity may be skipping a specific donation strategy that can reduce or eliminate their annual RMDs, according to Lawrence Pon, a CFP and CPA based in Redwood City, California.

They can use a so-called qualified charitable distribution, or QCD, Pon said.

A QCD has a dual tax benefit: It doesn't count as taxable income and may also satisfy at least part of an investor's yearly RMD, Pon said.

The move requires a direct transfer from an investor's pretax IRA to an eligible nonprofit organization. They can donate up to $111,000 in 2026. While QCD eligibility begins at age 70½, transfers won't start counting toward RMDs until an investor reaches their RMD age.

Generally, taxpayers only get a tax break for large charitable donations if they itemize deductions on their tax returns. But QCDs still provide a tax benefit for charitable gifts when investors use the standard deduction.

QCDs "provide a real benefit if you are not itemizing [tax deductions]," said Pon.

While there's no tax deduction for QCDs, the transfer won't increase a taxpayer's adjusted gross income, or AGI. Raising AGI can cause other tax consequences, such as higher premiums for Medicare Part B and Part D.
