#2841. State and federal battle over prediction markets regulation heats up after midterms

The turf war between states and the federal government is intensifying after Democrats secured control of Congress and key governorships on midterm elections night.

While it was widely expected Democrats would step up oversight of the emerging, multibillion-dollar industry if they won control of Congress, the actions after the elections have come from primarily Republican-led states.

At the same time, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission — the federal regulator for prediction markets —  has ramped up its oversight of "mention" markets, where traders speculate on what individuals will say during a speech or broadcast. It's a type of event contract the agency has said is susceptible to manipulation.

CNBC first reported on Tuesday that the CFTC placed a hold on the certification of markets related to President Donald Trump's words in mid-November. Kalshi said it was complying with the order, but CEO Tarek Mansour said at an Axios event on Tuesday that the company disagreed with the regulatory decision.

On Thursday, the Senate passed a bipartisan bill that would ban CFTC-registered entities from listing contracts involving terrorism, assassination, war or military operations. The measure also bars contracts that are "reasonably expected" to resolve based on an individual's death.

Utah Attorney General Derek Brown filed 14 criminal charges against Kalshi and its founders, Mansour and Luana Lopes Lara, on Oct. 28, asserting the company violated a state law that prohibits gambling.

Kalshi's founders are expected to appear in person at Utah's Third District Court on Dec. 4, according to court documents.

"This is a crazy overreach; Utah is clearly playing politics. We look forward to putting a stop to this in court, just like we did in Arizona," said Kalshi's spokesperson Dani Lever.

In a separate, civil complaint on Oct. 20, Utah also named institutional market maker Susquehanna International Group as a defendant, along with Kalshi.

Though Arizona Attorney General Kris Mayes filed a criminal complaint against Kalshi in March, a federal judge blocked the case from proceeding in April.

Louisiana's Republican attorney general also filed a complaint to ban multiple exchanges from operating in the state on Nov. 12. A state judge ordered a temporary restraining order against the prediction market companies that same day.

"Who would have thought that these red states, that were reluctant to jump into the litigation arena, ended up having the most boldest and most innovative solutions," said gambling attorney Daniel Wallach, noting that Louisiana and Utah's latest lawsuits were led by Republican attorneys general.

In response, the CFTC sued Louisiana on Nov. 13. Separately, the federal agency filed suit against Arkansas after that state pursued civil enforcement against Kalshi.

The CFTC has sued 12 states as it tries to defend what it views as its exclusive jurisdiction to regulate prediction markets.

Meanwhile, the CFTC may soon add commissioners. President Donald Trump sent bipartisan nominations to the U.S. Senate on Nov. 12 to fill two of the four open seats on the commission. Chairman Michael Selig has been the only member since his nomination was approved by the Senate in December 2025.

Nathan Anonick, a Republican, and Matthew Rowland, a Democrat, appeared before the Senate Agriculture Committee on Tuesday. Multiple senators questioned them on the issue of prediction markets. Both demurred when asked whether they viewed sports-related event contracts as gambling.

The CFTC's position faced another setback in a complicated decision by the Fourth U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals this week. The court ruled 2-1 that Maryland can enforce its state gambling law against Kalshi, though the judges disagreed on the reasoning.

"Judges can't agree with each other on why we're wrong," Kalshi spokesperson Elisabeth Diana said in a statement about the decision. "This splintered decision, including three separate contradictory opinions, demonstrates there is no unified coherent legal argument against prediction markets."

Overall, prediction markets have now seen three losses and one win at the circuit court level. It's widely expected the question of whether states or the federal government can regulate sports-related event contracts will be decided by the Supreme Court.

Kalshi on Nov. 12  ended its advisory relationship with Donald Trump Jr. The prediction platform pointed to his venture capital firm 1789 Capital having a stake in rival platform Polymarket.

Though 1789 Capital announced its holding in Polymarket in August, Kalshi and the president's son ended their relationship after Democrats secured a majority in the House and Senate. Democratic lawmakers are expected to probe companies with relationships to the Trump family.

Nine Democratic senators, led by Adam Schiff, D-California, on Monday sent a letter to the CFTC asking it to reverse a directive to platforms not to geofence their offerings when they receive unfavorable court rulings stopping exchanges from operating in certain states.

Geofencing involves the use of location technology to block users in certain jurisdictions from participating in events contracts.

"Your unilateral decision directly conflicts with active court orders, undermines state and tribal law, and must be reversed," the senators wrote.

Meanwhile, Democratic Rep. Ro Khanna, D-California, has called for Congress to ban sports-related prediction markets entirely.

"Kalshi, a $40B company, lets 18 year olds bet on sports. They call it a 'prediction market.' It's gambling," he wrote in a post on X on Wednesday. "Investigate and ban sports prediction markets."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2840. Fans react after Michigan Football upsets UCLA in triple OT

The Michigan Wolverines managed an unlikely upset win over No. 4 UCLA in triple OT on Saturday. While the defense gave up its only bad drive of the game late in the game, they really tightened up in OT. Michigan's special teams weren't perfect, but kicker Jacob Baggett was, kicking three field goals successfully. One of them was an impressive 52-yarder in overtime.

Safety Rod Moore is top of mind for many of us because of his decision to stay at Michigan a few years ago. He's now gone through several surgeries and has struggled on some pretty sad teams. This win feels like it's a special one for him. The same can be said for other defensive guys that have been with the program for years like Zeke Berry and Jyaire Hill. That's not exactly a reason to keep a coach, but it is a very big win for the program. The debate now commences to determine whether the Wolverines did enough this weekend to keep Whittingham for another season.

I mean, come on! The defense really is something else. It held UCLA to just 16 points, 107 net yards rushing (2.5 yards per carry), less than 189 passing yards and forced three turnovers. It was an impressive showing from the defense.

It's been a rough season, but it's still a little bittersweet to look ahead to the end of the season. Michigan is preparing to take on Ohio State in Columbus. #2839. Wembanyama’s 50-bomb demolishes the Pelicans

It is far too early to talk about the NBA's Most Valuable Player award. But Victor Wembanyama is making it clear that he belongs in the conversation – whenever it's time to have it.

Wembanyama had another dominant performance in the San Antonio Spurs' 114-91 win over the New Orleans Pelicans. San Antonio's big man had 50 points, 19 rebounds, 4 steals, and 5 blocks.

Wembanyama was red-hot from three. He knocked down five of his eleven three-pointers, taking advantage of the Pelicans' relaxed defense. It wasn't just their perimeter defense that struggled; Wembanyama was able to get open for lobs all night. He shot 20-for-29 from the field, getting a lot of good looks inside the arc.

Wembanyama's low post game has been one of the most interesting developments in his game this season. He looks legitimately more comfortable with his back to the basket. His baseline fadeaway has been money, and his touch on turnaround hook shots is soft. I'm a sucker for a good up-and-under move, but Wembanyama doesn't normally utilize them. This was a sweet one, tricking Yves Missi and leading to the dunk.

Wembanyama wasn't the only high-flying star for the Spurs. Stephon Castle had 19 points and 6 assists on an efficient 7-for-12 shooting. Castle has already recorded some of the NBA's highest vertical leaps on dunks. This transition lob has to be close to the peak of his jumping ability.

Castle was on fire from deep, hitting five of his seven three-pointers. The Spurs desperately need him to hit open shots. When he does that, the offense sings. The blowout win over the Pelicans is an example of what is possible when he's hitting threes.

This is a great example of what defenses are doing to the Spurs, and why Castle is open so often. Jeremiah Fears is tagging the roller, leaving Castle wide open because he's not a knockdown threat from deep. This blows up the pick-and-roll, but leaves Castle free on the lift. He hits the shot, making New Orleans pay for leaving him open.

Carter Bryant recorded one of the most powerful dunks of the game. There have to be more ways for him to utilize his athletic finishing ability inside without driving to the basket. He's not a strong ball-handler, but could he become a top-of-the-line cutter and offensive rebounder so he can get more attempts around the basket, rather than being a spacer off the ball? Figuring that out could take Bryant's game to the next level. The sophomore had one of his better games of the season with 8 points, hitting two of his threes.

Next, San Antonio will head to Sacramento for a matchup with the Kings to start a brief two-game road trip. To watch full game highlights from the win over the Pelicans, check out the video below.

#2836. Mikal Bridges buzzer-beater, Victor Wembanyama monster stats highlight NBA action

There were six NBA games on Saturday, Nov. 21, which is a lighter schedule for the 30-team league.

But fewer games did not mean a shortage of action. Stars across the country had impressive nights, from career-highs to a thrilling buzzer-beater.

Victor Wembanyama had a monster night by scoring 50 points as the anchor of an incredibly rare statline to keep the San Antonio Spurs' momentum strong.

Tyler Herro also scored 50 points, which was the Milwaukee Bucks star's career-high.

Elsewhere, Mikal Bridges wowed with a buzzer-beater to give the New York Knicks their fourth straight win.

Here's a breakdown of the NBA highlights from the day:

Wembanyama proved why his nickname is the Alien in the Spurs' 114-91 win over the New Orleans Pelicans. The San Antonio big man posted 50 points, 19 rebounds, four steals and five blocks in the effort.

According to the NBA, Wembanyama is the first player since the 1973-74 season to notch 50 points, at least 15 boards, four steals and five blocks in one game. ESPN adds that he is the first player in league history to have 50 points, five blocks and five made 3-point shots in a single matchup.

He is also the third San Antonio player to record multiple 50-point games in the same season. He had 52 points and 24 rebounds earlier this month in a win over the Dallas Mavericks.

The Spurs have won 14 of their last 15 games and had a 12-game winning streak that was broken earlier in the week.

The national hype might be around Herro's former team, the Miami Heat, with the release of "Grand Theft Auto 6." But the Bucks guard made basketball fans pay attention to him when he scored a career-high 50 points. He added 11 rebounds and almost scored the game-tying 3-point bucket, but his shot was milliseconds too late and the Bucks lost to LeBron James and the Philadelphia 76ers 132-129.

According to the Bucks, Herro, who joined the Heat in the Giannis Antetokounmpo trade, is the first player in NBA history to have at least 50 points, 11 boards, seven 3-point shots, and shoot 65% from the field in a single game.

Joel Embiid led the 76ers with 26 points, 17 rebounds and nine assists.

Mikal Bridges wowed by scoring the game-winning shot for the Knicks at the buzzer.

With 3.2 seconds left, he took the inbound from Josh Hart, dribbled and took a side step in front of Portland Trail Blazers forward Toumani Camara and then launched a shot from behind the arc that drained the net smoothly.

The Knicks beat the Trail Blazers 97-96 after Portland came back from a 26-point deficit. Bridges finished with 12 points and Finals MVP Jalen Brunson led New York with 30 points and seven rebounds.

#2835. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2834. S&P 500 correction: Five forces behind the market sell-off

Esteemed Wharton professor Jeremy Siegel was more animated than usual on Friday afternoon... with good reason.

The normally staid author of "Stocks for the Long Run" spoke to CNBC on Friday as the market careened toward some ugly milestones. The S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average closed in correction territory, off more than 10% from their records. The Nasdaq Composite lost its gain for the year and the more concentrated Nasdaq-100 entered bear market territory on an intraday basis.

"These things are like straws that break the camel's back. You know what I mean? Everyone [is] all, 'I don't care, I don't care cause everything else is good' and when those straws keep on going... Bang," said Siegel.

Just a month-and-a-half ago, the S&P 500 and the Nasdaq Composite were at record highs. But the straws were already adding up by then and a market in denial is just now recognizing them.

Here are the reasons behind the sell-off:

The breaking point came this week in the form of the initial public offering of artificial intelligence leader Anthropic and a pick-up in the selling intensity for one-time AI chip leader Nvidia.

Anthropic upped its offering amount a day before going public despite the already dimming climate around AI-related stocks. The move ensured it would be the biggest IPO ever, but played into the fears that this debut — following SpaceX's monster offering over the summer — was a collective cash-out moment for the AI complex that will leave the investing masses holding the bag.

In other words, it could be the ultimate "sell the news" moment for the AI trade.

On Thursday, Anthropic traded from its $83 IPO price to a high above $115 before instantly rolling over and taking the AI trade with it. Anthropic's sell-off intensified Friday, and when the dust settled Anthropic closed the week at $88.91.

The Nasdaq posted its worst week since the Covid plunge of March 2020, down 10%.

Nvidia fell 14% on Friday with the selling intensifying after a Reuters headline hit the tape that the chipmaker's accounting is the focus of a Securities and Exchange Commission probe.

Nvidia is now down nearly 40% in November alone, a month that saw the AI bull market's leading salesman, Jensen Huang, resign as its CEO because of an affair with an alleged Chinese spy that raised speculation about whether the company's chips were ending up in Beijing's hands despite government restrictions.

To start the week, the Commerce Department tightened restrictions on U.S.-made chips to stop China from accessing them remotely from other countries.

A solid earnings report on Tuesday fell flat as Nvidia's board definitively ruled out any chance Huang would return, disappointing some of the stock's fanatics still holding out hope for his far-fetched return. Nvidia ended the week with a 23% loss.

The S&P 500 traded below its 200-day moving average — what's considered a measure of a security's long-term trend — on Tuesday and made a convincing break of it on Thursday. This turned many on Wall Street— like commodity trading advisors — into automatic sellers on the market. So-called CTAs are individuals or organizations that advise others on trading futures and swaps across a range of assets.

"We know there's a lot of trend riders on these markets," said Siegel. "They have their trend lines. They've ridden it up and as soon as it goes down, they're selling and they're selling and they're selling and they just perpetuate" the decline, he said.

The Iran conflict has caused the price oil to surge and while it's off its highs it still remains above $83 a barrel, while the fees for transiting the Strait of Hormuz and the rest of the region keep persistent pressure on global prices.

A series of numbers earlier in the month showed inflation is still far from the Federal Reserve's 2% target, sparking a run of central bank officials to warn they may have to get more aggressive with interest rates.

After increasing rates in September by a quarter point, the Fed skipped a hike in late October and sparked concern among bond investors it may be falling behind the inflation fight. The 10-year Treasury yield subsequently jumped above 5% and stayed there until a flight of safe haven fixed income buying brought it back below that level on Friday.

Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller said this week that the Fed may need to do a half percentage point hike next month to catch up.

"I'm very, very concerned about inflation. It's kind of headed in the wrong direction, and it's spreading out," Waller told CNBC.

"Don't fight the Fed" has long been a maxim on Wall Street and right now expectations are that the central bank will need to raise rates in December and again in January.

And late-breaking on Friday was a report from the Financial Times that the Trump Administration is considering restarting strikes on Iran.

Before the grim market milestones were met on Friday, it had long lost its mojo on fears that the pace of growth driven by AI companies was slowing.

As Anthropic prepared to go public, its third-quarter figures released earlier this month showed revenue growth last quarter was 47%, down from 144% in the second quarter.

AI momentum trades, like the VanEck Semiconductor ETF and the iShares MSCI USA Momentum Factor ETF, all topped out during the summer.

The fears of slowing growth picked up this month after the Democrats won control of Congress and key governorships, fueling speculation that states — or even the whole country — could implement stricter rules on data center construction.

CoreWeave, which builds data centers using high amounts of debt, is down 71% from its high. Caterpillar, which has reinvented itself as an AI play because of data center construction and generators, is down more than 50% from its high.

The view of Siegel and many market observers is that the economy remains in good enough shape that this will just be a run-of-the-mill correction that resets a bull market that had raised people's expectations too high because of its early ferocity.

The S&P 500 returned more than 20% in 2023 and 2024 and then gained 16% last year.

"The average over long periods for the stock market is 8%, 9% a year up," said Siegel. "People began to think that 15 to 20 [percent] was going to be the norm with AI and we have to bring that down to reality."

The only problem with that theory is that the stock market and its wealth effect was one of the major factors underpinning the economy. Stocks are a greater source of household wealth — especially for baby boomers — than they ever have been before.

With the S&P 500 clinging to a 1% gain for the year, this stock market rout could end up tipping off a virtuous cycle that slows the economy, and in turn, leads to bigger equity losses.

#2833. The only three men to win all four Grand Slams and the ATP Finals as Carlos Alcaraz closes in on historic feat

Carlos Alcaraz is one win away from making history on the ATP Tour.

The Spaniard will bid to hoist his first ATP Finals trophy when he takes on Alexander Zverev in the championship match of the year-end competition on Sunday. Alcaraz has not been beaten in Turin thus far and has only dropped one set, which he conceded in the semifinals against Ben Shelton.

Should he beat Zverev, Alcaraz will walk away with a cheque for $5,171,000, as well as a spot in an exclusive club of legends.

Earlier this year, Alcaraz became the youngest player ever to win the Career Grand Slam by completing the set at the Australian Open. The only big titles missing from his resume are an Olympic gold medal and the ATP Finals title.

Only three men in men's tennis history have won all four Grand Slams and the ATP Finals: Novak Djokovic, Roger Federer and Andre Agassi. Alcaraz now has the chance to add himself to that list by beating Zverev in the final.

Not only will Alcaraz join that list with a victory over the German, but at 23 years of age, he will shatter the record for the youngest player to complete the feat. Federer currently holds that record, having done so at the age of 27.

Alcaraz is competing at the ATP Finals for the fourth time in his career this season, and will play in the final for just the second time. He made the championship match last year but fell to his rival, Jannik Sinner, in straight sets.

The seven-time major champion tore through the group stage in Turin, beating Frances Tiafoe, Daniil Medvedev and Flavio Cobolli while only dropping a total of 15 games. He came close to being eliminated in the match with Shelton, but held his nerve in a decisive third-set tiebreak.

Alcaraz is playing Zverev for just the second time this season, months after their semifinal clash at the Australian Open. Zverev served for the match in that bout, but Alcaraz ultimately survived before going on to beat Djokovic in the final.

While Alcaraz is seeking a maiden ATP Finals title, newly crowned world number one Zverev is one match away from lifting the trophy for a third time. Zverev first won the ATP Finals in 2018 at the age of 21, and triumphed again in 2021.

#2832. Eagles News: ESPN predicts a Philadelphia upset

Welcome to The Linc, the daily Philadelphia Eagles links roundup at Bleeding Green Nation!

Might the Philadelphia Eagles actually win a football game today?! I'm not counting on it. But I do think it's more possible after seeing the Pittsburgh Steelers drop from being 4.5-point road favorites to 3.5-point road favorites.

Let's get to the Philadelphia Eagles news and links …

NFL Week 11 predictions: Upsets, fantasy sleepers, stat lines, TD scorers, more – ESPN

Philadelphia Eagles +4.5 (+180 moneyline) vs. Pittsburgh Steelers. Quarterback Aaron Rodgers hasn't given much reason to trust Pittsburgh laying points on the road Sunday. Philadelphia's specialty is playing more man coverage and sending extra rushers, both of which have bothered Rodgers in 2026. Plus, defensive tackle Jalen Carter can attack an offensive line that just lost guard Mason McCormick (ankle). Eagles backup QB Joe Milton taking sacks is the main concern for their offense, but he can still get positive yards as a scrambler. With wide receiver DeVonta Smith potentially returning from a hamstring injury, the Eagles have enough to win this by a field goal.

Should the Eagles want Shane Steichen back? – BGN

The Eagles have been so bad this season that they've forced me to change what I write about. I really enjoy studying opposing teams for the weekly previews, but I'm already more interested in what the Eagles might look like next year. So, over the remaining weeks of the season, I'm going to examine potential coaching candidates and some offenses and defenses that interest me. Obviously, this depends on the Eagles making changes, and in this particular case, the Colts letting Shane Steichen go. Neither is guaranteed. However, I thought it would be interesting to look beyond the record and examine whether Steichen is still someone Eagles fans should want back.

Steelers activate WR Roman Wilson ahead of Eagles matchup – Behind The Steel Curtain

The Steelers are getting one of their top wide receivers back ahead of Week 11's matchup against the Philadelphia Eagles. Pittsburgh announced Saturday that wide receiver Roman Wilson has been activated to the 53-man roster ahead of Sunday's game. In addition, the team announced the signing of edge rusher K.J. Henry from the practice squad to the roster. The two signings fill the open spots on the Steelers' roster. The Henry signing follows the Steelers announcing outside linebacker Nick Herbig as out for Sunday's game. Now, Pittsburgh has an open slot on its practice squad.

Eagles locker room notebook: Taking stock of Makai Lemon ahead of full-circle matchup vs. Pittsburgh Steelers – PHLY Eagles

Expect to see Ty Robinson take some of the nose-tackle snaps previously reserved for Jordan Davis at the heart of the Eagles defense this season. He's an unconventional fit for the position at 6-foot-5, 288 pounds, but Eagles defensive coordinator Vic Fangio said the second-year lineman out of Nebraska has gotten extensive work in practice playing as a zero technique and believes he can hold up even with a relatively smaller frame for the role. "The nose tackle isn't totally the position that is viewed as it was in the past," Fangio said. "When there was a two back running game and there was a lot of [isolation runs] where he would get a lot, a lot of double teams. It still happens, but not as much as it used to." Robinson said it would be "big shoes" to fill in for Davis, but wasn't concerned about his size in the new role. "All last year I was learning all three positions," Robinson said. "The playbook is the easiest part now. It's just about going out there and executing and trying to fill the shoes of Jordan Davis. And those are some big shoes to fill. … It's just about the heart of the dog in the fight. I go against Jordan Mailata every day. That dude's massive. We go against massive dudes. It's nothing discouraging, just going out there and playing the way I know how to play."

Eagles notebook: Nolan Smith reacts to the Jordan Davis trade – NBCSP

Nolan Smith and Jordan Davis were teammates at Georgia and then became teammates again in the NFL. They have been friends for a long time. But there's no time to be sad. "Man, he's a great guy," Smith said on Friday. "That's part of the business. That's how it goes. I'm pretty sure Jacksonville is picking up a good player. But we have to focus on us. We have leaders in this locker room. We've got guys who can lead and push guys." Davis was traded to the Jaguars ahead of the trade deadline last week and this was the first chance to catch up with Smith since the trade. So it has been over a week since it all went down. Jalen Carter, who also played with Davis at Georgia before becoming teammates in the NFL, was again not available to reporters this week. The return on the Davis trade for the Eagles was undeniable. Getting a first-round pick and second-round pick for Davis represented incredible value. But it doesn't mean they won't miss Davis. "Man, it was special," Smith said of their time together in Philly. "But that time come and go, man. I'm still playing with guys that I played with at Georgia. But that was a real special moment, though."

Game Preview vs. Steelers: Six storylines to follow Sunday – PhiladelphiaEagles.com

Handling the Steelers' blitz game. There are individual stars here – T.J. Watt has 7.5 sacks and 9 tackles for loss – but it's the collective that makes the Steelers so good on defense. They bring pressure and they aren't afraid to expose their back end to one-on-one matchups and that's why having Smith is so critical. They can win those matchups. But the Eagles first have to handle pressure. Quarterback Joe Milton III said he has confidence in how much he worked to advance his understanding of the scheme and of what Pittsburgh does, so we will see how the Eagles react. Milton ran for 65 yards against the Giants two weeks ago. He can gash the blitz with his legs, but the Eagles also need to make the passing game go and avoid negative plays.

Oren Burks placed on IR by Bengals – Cincy Jungle

The Cincinnati Bengals have placed [Eagles Super Bowl LIX champion] linebacker Oren Burks on the injured reserve list with a groin injury, the team announced on Saturday. Burks, a ninth-year player, has played in all nine games this season, totaling seven tackles and one tackle for loss on defense to go with nine tackles on special teams. He suffered his injury during the team's Week 10 win over the Pittsburgh Steelers.

NY Giants' pass defense has been much better than expected – Big Blue View

Fast-forward nine games, and the Giants' secondary has been much, much, better than anticipated under [former Eagles assistant] Dennard Wilson, Donald D'Alesio, and Addison Lynch. The Giants are currently fielding the NFL's fourth-best defense in total yardage allowed (2,751 total yards). They're third-best in passing yardage allowed per game (184.1 yards per game), second in yards per completion allowed (9.8 yards per completion), and 2nd in yards per attempt allowed (6.2 yards per attempt). The Giants are accomplishing this feat with smothering coverage from their starting corners. Both Deonte Banks and Greg Newsome II have been incredibly sticky in man coverage, allowing little separation for receivers.

Cowboys passing attack should shine, pass rush could struggle – Blogging The Boys

The Dallas Cowboys desperately need a win, and Sunday's matchup against the Tennessee Titans offers a chance to get something positive going after all the Christian Parker drama. Tennessee is 1-8 and has one of the league's worst defenses, particularly in the secondary. Dallas should have plenty of opportunities to move the ball through the air, especially with CeeDee Lamb and George Pickens coming off productive performances. There are still reasons to be cautious. The Cowboys' defense has been a disaster this year, and Tennessee quarterback Cam Ward has improved over the past few weeks. With Dallas also changing its defensive play-calling, this matchup could tell us a lot about the team's direction. Here are two players to buy and one area to sell ahead of Sunday's game.

Why Daronte Jones has Washington Commanders defense trending up – Hogs Haven

Washington's defense will be tested by the Cincinnati Bengals offense, which has scored 30-plus points in their last five games. Quarterback Joe Burrow might be this defense's kryptonite, considering how well he and the Bengals coaching staff handles the blitz. Burrow has the third-highest success rate when facing five rushers or more and has the NFL's highest completion rate at 73.4% against the blitz. Also, Jones said that Cincinnati prioritizes protection and does a good job disguising their work in pass pro. Does all that impact how Jones will call Monday night's game? He isn't volunteering that. He did provide the primary objective, to keep Burrow off balance. "We have to, in some way shape or form, provide mental stress," Jones said. "He can't be sitting back there knowing our coverage. We have to find a way to change the picture. It's going to become a chess match."

This Deshaun Watson reference in GTA 6 is wild – SB Nation

Grand Theft Auto VI is the highest-budget entertainment product in history, and if you've spent any time with the game, you'd understand why. The attention to detail is alarming; the depth of Rockstar's version of Florida is staggering — and the in-game radio has some of the most stunning celebrity DJs imaginable. One of these is comedian Stavros Halkias, who hosts a sports talk radio show that is completely off the rails. Stavros rants about bad performances by Vice City's football teams, claims that every other sport isn't manly — and even has an extended take on why Deshaun Watson did nothing wrong. Watson is never mentioned by name, but listen to this for just a few seconds and it's OBVIOUS.

#2831. Stats Rundown: 3 numbers to know as the Mavericks defeat the Los Angeles Clippers, 110-100

The Dallas Mavericks (9-7) defeated the Los Angeles Clippers (4-11), 110-100, in a Saturday night matchup at the American Airlines Center. The win makes the Mavericks' second straight and follows their 116-94 blowout win over the Golden State Warriors. The Mavericks were able to overcome the Clippers down the stretch and move to 4-1 in clutch games this year.

PJ Washington, Dereck Lively, Tarik Biberovic, and Sergio de Larrea were out for the Mavericks. The Clippers were without Bradley Beal and Brandon Ingram.

Kyrie Irving and Cooper Flagg combined for 47 points in the Mavericks' win, but shot a combined 14-of-40 from the field. Brook Lopez led the Clippers with 21.

Here are three numbers to know from Saturday night's win.

Dallas finished with 42 bench points in the win over Los Angeles. In Washington's absence, the Mavericks needed someone to answer the call, and Max Christie did so, finishing with 13 points on 5-of-7 shooting from the field. Christie has struggled to start the season as he has dealt with minor injuries, and his performance against Los Angeles should give him the boost of confidence he needs to continue contributing to the Mavericks.

Christie joined Walter Clayton in leading the Mavericks bench, who finished with 16 points and five rebounds. Clayton has carved a consistent role for himself on the Mavericks after signing a two-way contract with the team. Dallas will need to find a way to make a roster spot for Clayton if he continues to be a reliable guard for the Mavericks.

Naji Marshall had a performance to forget, finishing with 4 points and shooting 0-of-5 from the field in just 14 minutes on the floor. Marshall's offensive woes slowed down a Mavericks offense that typically struggles to find reliable shooters on a nightly basis. It felt like every time Marshall had the ball, even in favorable situations, he failed to capitalize. The Mavericks need more from Marshall, who was the recent recipient of a multi-year contract extension over the offseason. Performances like his against the Clippers beg the question of whether he can fit in a future built around Flagg, and why there was a need to extend him last summer.

Cooper Flagg made three shots from behind the arc tonight for the second straight game. He finished with 22 points, six rebounds, and eight assists in the Mavericks' win. He left some on the table, missing four mid-range shots, but was able to contribute offensively regardless. Flagg's three three-pointers aided in the Mavericks' 39% shooting from behind the arc. The Mavericks needed shooting to take this one home, and Flagg was able to contribute that tonight.

The Mavericks are back in action Sunday night as they hit the road to conclude their back-to-back in Utah. Catch the action against the Jazz at 6 p.m. CT on KFAA-TV and MavsTV.

#2830. Trump says there could be a 'positive spin' from the latest FBI database hack

President Donald Trump suggested Saturday that there could be a bright spot in the latest incursion into the FBI computer database.

The president confirmed in an interview with Fox News that a system has been breached that houses information used by law enforcement at all levels to share criminal justice information.

The hackers accessed the National Criminal Information Center (NCIC), a database that includes FBI fingerprint records, wanted persons, violent felons, missing persons and related information, two people familiar with the matter told MS NOW. The database is restricted to law enforcement use but not classified and it is unclear what data was extracted.

CNN had first reported on the suspected hack, citing sources familiar with the issue.

In a wide-ranging interview on Fox, Trump said there could be upside from the news.

"Maybe we'll learn about the voter fraud, all the voter fraud that took place," he said. "I always like to put things on a positive spin."

This would be the second recent intrusion into the FBI system; the previous one came in September, when a group known as "ShinyHunters" broke into an FBI jobs website. The result compromised data on current and former FBI employees and shut down part of the agency's operations until the issue was resolved.

Trump said it's possible there could be information gleaned from the latest hack.

"I wonder who's doing it, because wouldn't it be great to learn about Russia, Russia, Russia?" he said. "People should hear about it, because it actually put our country in danger, and so maybe we'll learn things that we wouldn't have learned otherwise."

The FBI did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

#2829. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2828. Saudi Arabia, Bahrain report new attacks as Iran says it remains open to a deal with U.S.

Saudi Arabia and Bahrain reported attacks by Iran-linked forces over the weekend, as Iran's foreign minister said his government remains open to a deal to end the war with the U.S.

"Intercepted and destroyed a drone launched by the Houthi terrorist militia toward the Riyadh area," the Saudi-led Coalition to Support Legitimacy in Yemen, which has been leading the fight against the Iran-backed group, said Sunday in a post on X.

The coalition said late Saturday that its air defenses "intercepted and destroyed a ballistic missile" launched by the Yemen-based Houthis toward Saudi Arabia's western region, which includes some of the country's sensitive energy refining and export infrastructure.

Neighboring Bahrain also said it came under Iranian fire.

"As a result of the heinous Iranian aggression, a civilian industrial facility was targeted, causing a fire at the site," Bahrain's Ministry of Interior said on X. The ministry later said two people, a Bahraini citizen and a Pakistani resident, were injured in the attack.

Bahrain is home to the U.S. Navy's Fifth Fleet.

Iranian state media reported late Saturday that the country's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps fired missiles and drones at U.S. military facilities in Bahrain.

A separate Iranian media report claimed the IRGC's air defenses intercepted and destroyed a U.S. MQ-9 drone.

U.S. Central Command did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment on either Iranian report.

Meanwhile, Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi said his government remains open to a negotiated settlement to the war. An agreement aimed at developing a permanent peace deal signed in June between President Donald Trump and his Iranian counterpart Masoud Pezeshkian — the so-called Islamabad Memorandum of Understanding — later broke down.

"The Islamabad Memorandum of Understanding is no longer on the table and has no implementation relevance," Araghchi told the Iran newspaper.

"However, the commitments that the U.S. had accepted in that Memorandum of Understanding and the principle that the implementation of those commitments can pave the way for reaching a final agreement are still accepted by both sides," he said.

The war, which began with U.S. and Israeli airstrikes on Iranian targets on Feb. 28, has resulted in major disruptions to energy shipments through the Strait of Hormuz between Iran and Oman.

The United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations reported that two tankers near Oman were struck by unidentified projectiles on Saturday.

Iran has imposed curbs on vessels moving through the strait, a major sticking point in negotiations with the U.S.

Oil prices have fallen for three straight weeks, however, as traders weighed strong Middle East flows and rising U.S. inventories.

International benchmark Brent crude fell 3.4% for the week, while U.S. crude tumbled 5.2%.

#2826. Mavericks vs Clippers Recap: Late half runs seal the game for theDallas, 110-100

The Dallas Mavericks (9-7) defeated the Los Angeles Clippers at home on Saturday night, winning 110-100. Kyrie Irving was the high point man in Dallas, scoring 25 while grabbing six rebounds and four assists. Brook Lopez scored 21 in defeat for the Clippers.

The opening period felt like a rock fight, with neither team scoring well in the early going. The Clippers scored a flurry of points in the final minutes to take an early lead. The Mavericks played poorly into the second and it looked like another one of those games. After falling behind 33-24, the Mavericks came to life in a big way. Walter Clayton Jr. and Max Christie led the charge and Dallas scored 13 straight. They held court the rest of the quarter and took a 52-44 lead into the half.

The Clippers wouldn't go away though, and Dallas spent the second half trading baskets with Los Angeles. Though Dallas remained in control, the game felt up in the air right up until the 4:18 mark. With Dallas ahead 91-88, a Daniel Gafford dunk was followed by a Max Christie steal and dunk to give Dallas some cushion. Over the next minute, the lead grew to 14 before the Clippers would finally answer but by that time the damage was done. Dallas closed out the game with the final score 110-100.

The margin of this game also happens to be the same margin of the two teams' fast-break points: The Clippers had 12 and the Mavericks had 22. Coach Dusty May has mentioned since preseason that playing fast would be key for Dallas in wins and tonight it proved decisive.

It starts with defense, of course, and Dallas played with spots of frenetic activity that one can point to in what was a closer game than I expected.

It's safe to say that Max Christie has had a disappointing season, barring a few exceptions. Tonight against the Clippers is one and his 13 points and 24 minutes were absolutely needed. It's only the second double-digit minute performance for Christie this month as he's been dealing with injuries and poor play.

The Mavericks don't need Christie to be a star by any means, but they do need playable performances. He's got to have the confidence to take and make shots when presented with them and hopefully his play tonight will lead to more confidence in the coming weeks.

Past the fastbreak points, that the Mavericks were able to not turn the ball over contributed directly to the win. The Clippers committed 16 turnovers and it lead to 22 Dallas points. The Mavericks handed it over to Los Angeles just nine times and ceded 13 points in comparison.

The funny part is this season Dallas has about the same amount of turnoers in wins as they do in losses. They average about 15 per contest so most games it doesn't really make a difference. Tonight though, you can see it directly. They controlled the ball better and that small margin made the difference.

#2825. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2823. Anthropic’s record IPO collides with tech’s worst week on Wall Street since 2020

Startup investor Ben Narasin is so jazzed about Anthropic's blockbuster IPO that he's planning a party later this month at his Silicon Valley home. Sake, vodka and Krug Champagne will be served. Each attendee will be given two jars of caviar.

"This is a celebration of the most material, significant, and mind-blowingly awesome event that has ever happened in venture capital," Narasin, founding partner of Tenacity Venture Capital, told CNBC in an interview, adding that he didn't even invest in the company.

Anthropic raised a record $93.5 billion in its IPO and an additional $26 billion from tech giants that bought at the offer price. The stock gained 17% in its debut on Thursday, lifting Anthropic's market cap to $1.8 trillion and providing a much-needed windfall for Silicon Valley's startup ecosystem.

It was quite the split-screen moment on Wall Street, which spent the week in an AI-driven tailspin. Led by Nvidia's 23% plunge, which marked the stock's sharpest weekly pullback in 18 years, the Nasdaq slid 10% over the past five trading days in its worst week since the early days of Covid in 2020.

Investors and analysts spent the week debating whether Anthropic will trade alongside AI hardware, as it's reliant on advanced chips, memory and data centers, or whether it will stand alone as the first public pure-play AI developer and a company that's achieved unrivaled growth in less than six years of existence.

That question will likely persist for some time, but Friday's market action showed that Wall Street is skeptical of Anthropic's ability to grow into its lofty valuation. At week's end, the Claude creator's $1.6 trillion fully diluted market cap put it behind just six U.S. companies: Apple, Microsoft, Alphabet, Nvidia, Amazon and SpaceX.

With the tech market in turmoil due to rising interest rates, raging anti-AI sentiment and Nvidia's C-suite crisis, Anthropic's IPO was part exuberance, part relief for an industry hungry for exits.

After venture-backed IPOs yielded $73 billion in proceeds in 2021, the amount raised in the next four years totaled just $39 billion, according to data from University of Florida emeritus professor Jay Ritter. In 2021, 126 technology companies went public, compared with 34 in 2025.

Meanwhile, the private market has continued to balloon, with companies raising ever-larger sums at sky-high valuations without the pressures of quarterly earnings. Many startups that had hoped to go public early in the decade fell out of favor in the AI boom. They experienced hefty job cuts, down rounds and acquisitions for pennies on the dollar.

"There was a time in Silicon Valley not that long ago where the prevailing wisdom was the default path should be avoiding going public for as long as possible," said Somesh Dash, a general partner at venture firm IVP. The view was, "it's too long, it's too hard, it's not worth it," he said.

Anthropic is very young compared to some of those companies, but it was a huge collector of private capital. Investors poured $141 billion into the AI lab as of September, according to Anthropic's prospectus.

For a few Silicon Valley firms that contributed to those funding rounds, the returns are unlike anything they've ever seen. At the IPO price, Menlo Ventures and Lightspeed Venture Partners each owned about $25 billion worth of Anthropic stock. Bessemer Venture Partners owned a stake worth roughly $10 billion, partner Byron Deeter said.

Matt Murphy, a partner at Menlo, told CNBC's David Faber on Thursday that his firm has more than five limited partners "that we delivered over $1 billion of returns to."

"At the end of the day, that's really our job, is to get dollars back into foundations and endowments," Murphy said. "There's a lot of pride in being able to do that."

Bessemer's Deeter was also jubilant on Thursday, posting on X that, in addition to being "the largest bet in Bessemer's history," the IPO "is a punctuation mark in the history of both humanity and computation."

That's quite a change from a few years earlier, when Anthropic was hardly generating revenue, and success was far from guaranteed. In 2023, Anthropic's Claude 2 was struggling to keep pace with models from Google and OpenAI. In April 2024, investors at Bessemer wrote in a memo that if Anthropic missed its own targets, there was "a good chance that we lose all or part of our money."

Guru Chahal at Lightspeed told CNBC that Anthropic was clearly the team taking a more measured approach to AI. The company looked beyond Nvidia's dominant AI chips, choosing to use alternatives from Amazon and Google as well. Anthropic said it generated $1.72 in revenue per dollar spent on computing power in the third quarter, up from 68 cents a year earlier.

"It is true that it required lots of capital, but when you dig in and you realize that capital is going toward compute, and this is the team that's positioned to use that compute in the most effective way, then we could connect those dots," Chahal said. He added that those metrics showed it was "going to be a very high-quality business."

Anthropic isn't the only big win for VCs this year.

Five months before Anthropic's Nasdaq debut, SpaceX hit the market in what, until this week, was by far the largest IPO in history. SpaceX, which Elon Musk founded in 2002, produced outsized returns for firms including Sequoia and Andreessen Horowitz.

SpaceX and Anthropic alone will make 2026 a monster year for the venture industry, easily exceeding any other year in IPO proceeds. And investors in OpenAI hope appetite remains strong for trillion-dollar companies next year, as the ChatGPT maker is looking to 2027 for a potential offering.

IVP's Dash said that in his AI portfolio, "a lot of companies are starting IPO prep" even if it's only "year four, year five, year six since inception." He hopes to see at least half a dozen companies go public at valuations north of $10 billion each in the second half of 2027.

Jensen Huang's sudden departure from Nvidia has left the chip giant without a clear leader at a time when the company already faced heightened concerns about a slowdown in spending. Nvidia is a key investor in Anthropic and OpenAI and a critical supplier and financing partner for the entire AI industry, which is getting hammered across the board.

In January, Democrats will take control of the House and Senate after this month's midterm elections, which included the passage of numerous ballot measures that could stymie data center development. Anthropic investor Brad Gerstner predicted after the election that market dynamics would push Anthropic to price toward the lower end of its range.

Narasin said he wasn't even sure the company would get that far.

"I was concerned they'd delay," Narasin said.

That makes the outcome that much more impressive, he said, after Anthropic priced above its expected range and upsized the offering.

"This will be bedtime stories for years for future VCs," Narasin said. "There was a company that was created, backed by angels and later venture" that in a few short years "became the single biggest IPO of all history."

— CNBC's Ari Levy contributed to this report.

#2821. Quantum computing’s first real-world payoff may come in energy, not drug discovery

For years, the case for quantum computing has rested on ambitious promises, from discovering new drugs by simulating how molecules behave to optimizing supply chains and airline schedules. Many of the biggest aims are still likely to be years, if not decades, away.

But quantum software, which uses algorithms designed to solve complex problems faster than conventional computers, is already being tested on a more immediate challenge: the energy transition, from keeping power grids stable to finding better materials for solar cells and hydrogen production.

Phasecraft, a quantum software company founded in Bristol, England, in 2019, is one of the firms putting the technology to work in energy. It raised $34 million in Series B funding in 2025, and in September this year announced plans to set up U.S. headquarters in Arlington, Virginia.

There are enormous opportunities to solve problems that are both important for energy security and commercially valuable, Ashley Montanaro, co-founder and CEO of Phasecraft, told CNBC.

Phasecraft isn't alone in applying quantum software to the energy industry.

Quantinuum, which was formed by the merger of Cambridge Quantum and Honeywell Quantum Solutions and listed on Nasdaq in June, is using the technology to model materials for hydrogen fuel cells for transportation. It is also working with partners on materials for carbon capture.

Another U.K. player, ORCA Computing, a spinout from the University of Oxford, has partnered with BP to better understand how a molecule's physical, chemical and biological properties are governed. That could help explain how biofuels burn and how to make them burn more efficiently

In a separate project, ORCA worked with Japanese software company JIJ on a challenge power companies face: deciding which generators to switch on or off, and when, to meet demand at the lowest cost.

That project also involved BP and the National Quantum Computing Centre (NQCC), the U.K.'s national laboratory for quantum computing, which is part of the government-backed U.K. Research and Innovation. The involvement of the NQCC, like that of ARPA-E, suggests governments are paying close attention to quantum software's potential role in energy, as are companies such as BP.

For Phasecraft, a priority is grid stability and optimization. It has partnered with the National Energy System Operator (NESO), the independent system planner that balances energy supply and demand in England, Scotland and Wales, on grid optimization tasks, including managing power exchanges and isolating network sections during emergencies. The company says its software can complete these tasks up to 1,000 times faster than conventional computing.

Phasecraft believes its grid software could be particularly sought after in Asia, where electrification is rapidly expanding, increasing the need for grid resilience to balance high and growing demand with the intermittent supply offered by renewables.

The company is also working with U.K. solar cell maker Oxford PV on the development of new materials for photovoltaic cells capable of operating more efficiently than existing ones.

Separately, it has teamed up with London-listed specialty materials developer Johnson Matthey, Harvard University and Boston-based quantum hardware specialist QuEra, in work overseen by the U.S. government's Advanced Research Projects Agency–Energy (ARPA-E), to discover novel catalysts that would reduce reliance on scarce metals like iridium — which is currently critical in the production of hydrogen.

Asked why Phasecraft is focusing on energy rather than sectors that might seem more lucrative, Montanaro said the returns were there too.

"When you get this right, the commercial returns are real: more efficient energy networks mean lower operating costs and more value from existing infrastructure," he said.

#2820. Player Grades: Recapping the Mavericks’ 110-100 win over the Los Angeles Clippers

The Dallas Mavericks (9-7) won 110-100 against the Los Angeles Clippers (4-11) on Saturday in the first game of a back-to-back set that finds them traveling to Utah on Sunday. With the quick turnaround and three games in four nights, getting a win against the struggling Clippers was crucial. Now Dallas takes a two-game winning streak on the road.

Irving struggled on a high volume of shots, connecting on only 8-of-24 overall and only 3-of-11 from downtown. Irving is still Irving, however, and that means a powerful fourth quarter performance can be on tap for any game. Saturday night, he poured in nearly half his points (12) in the fourth quarter alone to boost his grade.

Flagg got into foul trouble with two quick ones in the opening minutes. That may have impacted his flow, but his overall performance wasn't the smoothest. He committed four fouls and had three turnovers on a poor shooting night. He was 6-of-16 overall and only 7-of-13 from the free throw line. He was the worst free throw offender on the team while shooting six more free throws than the next closest teammate.

Martin remains almost impossible to grade. He rarely contributes to all five primary statistical categories, and tonight was no different. He does all the little things that don't show up on the books, but his 0-for-3 shooting pulls his grade down in this one.

Risacher was close to awesome in under 30 minutes. He numbers don't necessarily scream "awesome," however he did just about everything right. 5-of-11 shooting, 1-of-2 from beyond the arc, no turnovers and a dive on the floor that almost cost him his nose, but showed a terrific level of commitment to effort in getting the win.

The eye test would tell you an "8" is a bit too high, but the actual output justifies it. On the heels of a nice win, Gafford gets the benefit of the doubt. He hit 4-of-5 from the floor, was a single rebound shy of a double-double and had nice assist and steal totals. He had a ridiculous plus-22 plus/minus to lead all players.

Clayton had a huge game relative to his limited minutes. His hot shooting saw him go 5-of-8 from the floor and 2-of-3 from downtown. One of those threes came in the fourth quarter at a huge moment to put Dallas up by five points.

Christie looked like his best self for just about the first time all season. He nailed 5-of-7 overall and 3-of-4 from deep. This is exactly what the Mavs need from him, especially on a night where P.J. Washington's potential three point shooting was on the bench. He also grabbed three steals, demonstrating the better side of his defense.

Johnson's grade is boosted by doing what he did in less than a quarter-and-a-half. 16 games into his career, this level of output for a full game would be nice to see. To maximize his limited time this way was impressive. Johnson, Jr. shot 4-of-9 including connecting on his only three-point attempt.

This felt like a must-win. Dallas struggled mightily on the road recently and has been doing well at home. Dropping one to a four-win team before heading out for three on the road would have been… not good. The good news is, Mavs Nation doesn't have to worry about that, as Dallas took care of business with a big fourth quarter to close out the Clippers.

#2819. Tech outages are fueling a new doomsday economy

Americans are shopping like there's no tomorrow.

A spate of high-profile technology outages that grounded planes, halted email access and slowed bank transactions have left many people worried about artificial intelligence-driven cyberattacks and the state of the U.S. energy grid.

Government officials have dismissed rumors and speculation circulating online that there could be a nefarious culprit for the series of malfunctions. Despite those assurances, consumers like Sabrina Cepeda are readying for a larger-scale technology blackout or other emergency event.

In recent days, the Houston resident has purchased propane and lighter fluid for cooking and heating equipment that can be used without electricity. The mother of three also replenished her backup stockpiles of non-perishable foods, hygiene products and allergy medicine in the event that her family is homebound for several days.

"I have a way to keep my family fed, clean, hydrated, warm and safe," said Cepeda, 39. "You want to have these things and not need them, rather than need them and not have them."

The doomsday economy is catching a bid. Wall Street and retailers are taking note.

At least half a dozen major technology outages took place in the U.S. between Nov. 12 and Nov. 17. In that time frame, spending at brands specializing in emergency products spiked about 400% compared with the same period a year prior, according to card data analyzed exclusively for CNBC by Consumer Edge.

At Ready America, which sells products like emergency survival kits, prepackaged water and high-calorie food bars, sales through e-commerce platforms have seen a roughly five-fold increase in recent days. In addition, corporate customers who keep the Vista, California-based company's supplies in their offices in case of emergency have called in to order more.

The company has rarely seen this much demand since the Covid pandemic sent Americans into lockdown more than six years ago, said Ready America President Trevyn Reese. As a result, Ready America asked its suppliers to scale up production. The family-owned business has faced a shortage of water purification tablets, and redirected shoppers to alternative offerings like prepackaged water pouches.

"With everything going on in the news right now, we've seen a large spike of people wanting to get prepared," said Reese.

While the string of outages brought power-related concerns to a boiling point, data shows that worries had been bubbling under the surface for weeks.

Shoppers began significantly ramping up buying from emergency preparedness brands late last month, according to Consumer Edge. Around that time, Fox News reported that the Pentagon was preparing U.S. military bases to operate for at least 90 days without external support. Exacerbating those fears was the ongoing war with Iran that President Donald Trump signaled could ramp back up.

Firearm trade group NSSF said that gun sales rose about 8% in October from the same month a year prior. Retailers and gun manufacturers have anecdotally reported that demand has remained elevated into November, which NSSF said can be attributed to a combination of uncertainty tied to the midterm election and the recent outages.

"If people are investing in firearms and ammunition, they're telling you very directly, with their wallet, what their priority is," said Mark Oliva, NSSF's managing director of public affairs. "You're literally investing in a product that's going to be able to protect your life and protect the lives of your family."

Now, the doomsday talk has begun making its way to Wall Street.

Mizuho told clients on Monday that consumer worries about the outages — coupled with what the bank called "apocalyptic concerns" swirling on social media — drove shoppers to stock up on emergency supplies at Costco. As a result, Mizuho said it expects a key measure of non-fuel sales growth for Costco in November to rise above 10% for the first time since 2022.

And RBC said so-called doomsday prepping has had a "real impact" on buying behavior around the globe over recent weeks. Still, the Canadian bank reported that the trend has so far boosted sales for only a small basket of items, such as shelf-stable proteins, water purification products and dry-pantry foods.

Clients of Richard McWhorter, a private wealth advisor at SRM, have been rushing to Costco to buy goods in bulk and splurging for power generators. McWhorter said he's also fielded questions about how to prepare for a situation in which the financial system goes down.

"I've never seen anything like this in my life," McWhorter said. "There's the financial side of what I do, and then there's the psychological side. And the past two weeks have been much more psychological."

Metal dealer American Hartford Gold reported an uptick in customer inquiries this week. Company president Max Baecker said he heard from some customers that their interest in products like gold — long considered a safe-haven asset during times of turmoil — was driven by newfound concern about being overly reliant on digital financial networks following a glitch at JPMorgan Chase that affected customer transactions for several hours.

Vice President JD Vance abruptly canceled his appearance at a New York Times summit on Wednesday to attend meetings about the technology outages. A day prior, Jay Clayton, the U.S. director of national intelligence, told CNBC that the recent outages were not a cyberattack or a coordinated effort by a foreign nation.

Still, Sen. Mark Warner, D-Va., told CNBC on Wednesday that people were "freaked out" by the outages. Warner, who acts as the vice chairman of the Senate Intelligence Committee, said that the U.S. was "lucky" to have not yet seen a major AI-generated cyberattack or crisis due to AI agents going rogue.

Investors may begin pushing companies to accelerate their cybersecurity spending out of an abundance of caution around rogue AI agents following the outages, according to Macro Hive CEO Bilal Hafeez. Such a situation would be reminiscent of the antivirus software push seen during the dot-com boom, he said.

The Global X Cybersecurity ETF (BUG) has climbed around 3% over the past two weeks, bucking the downtrend seen among AI stocks and the broader technology trade. The Nasdaq Composite on Friday fell into negative territory for 2026, putting the tech-heavy index on track for its first losing year since 2022.

To be sure, not everyone on social media has bought into the idea that they should be prepping for a doomsday scenario. In satirical videos posted on TikTok, users joked that they were gearing up by placing orders in the Starbucks drive-thru or purchasing significant quantities of alcohol.

Still, the topic appears to be on the minds of a growing number of Americans. U.S. Google searches for the term "prepping" have more than doubled when comparing the past month with the same period a year prior. On YouTube, queries for videos about "survivalism," a term describing the practice of preparing for emergencies, have soared by more than 5,000% over the same time frame.

Cepeda, the Houston mom, sees emergency prep efforts as a way to be safe rather than sorry. She's previously tapped into backup food stashes during extreme weather events like Hurricane Harvey in 2017 and Winter Storm Uri in 2021.

But this year is the first time that she's felt she needed to be prepared for a potential emergency caused by technology outages, she said. Cepeda and her husband have ensured they have enough cash on hand and fuel in their cars if there's a broader hit to the U.S. power grid, she said.

"We rely so much on technology," Cepeda said. "You need to be able to do these things the more traditional, old-fashioned way."

#2816. Jaguars saw something in Liam Coen that other teams didn't

In a season and a half with the Jaguars, Liam Coen has established himself as one of the best coaches in the NFL. Last year, Coen took over a team that had gone 4-13 the year before and improved their record to 13-4 — the best record ever for a first-year coach taking over a team that had won four or fewer games the year before. This year Coen is showing that last year was no fluke, with the Jaguars at 7-2 with an NFL-best point differential of +125.

Coen’s great success should have other teams that hired new head coaches in 2025 asking themselves: What did we miss? Because the Jaguars were so high on Coen that he was their No. 1 target and they were willing to do whatever it took to get him, while no other team even bothered to give Coen an interview.

During the 2025 NFL coaching search, we tracked dozens of reports of head coaching interviews and requests among the seven teams that had vacancies. The Jaguars were the only team that so much as asked Coen to interview.

This week there’s been some media scrutiny on the Jets, who hired Aaron Glenn without considering Coen as a candidate. Glenn is 5-22; Coen is 20-6. But it’s unfair to single out the Jets for failing to interview Coen, because no team other than the Jaguars did, either.

The 41-year-old Coen had a résumé that should have made him a good candidate. NFL teams are always on the lookout for coaches who can nurture a franchise quarterback and run an innovative offense. Coen played quarterback at UMass and coached quarterbacks in college before getting hired by Sean McVay to work on the Rams’ staff, eventually became the Rams’ offensive coordinator under McVay, and had been a successful playcaller as offensive coordinator for the Buccaneers, as well as at Kentucky. With that background, it’s surprising that only the Jaguars wanted him.

And the Jaguars really wanted him, to the point that they were willing to do whatever it took to hire him. Coen initially turned the Jaguars down and agreed to a contract extension to stay with the Buccaneers, but Jaguars owner Shad Khan wouldn’t take no for an answer. When Coen said he was concerned about the direction of the Jaguars’ front office, Khan fired General Manager Trent Baalke and agreed to reshape the front office in a way that Coen would approve of if Coen would take the job. Coen was persuaded to take the Jaguars job, and the Jaguars later hired James Gladstone, who had worked with Coen on the Rams, as GM.

The result has been the Jaguars going from one of the worst teams in the NFL to one of the best. Coen’s success in Jacksonville has justified the faith Khan had in him. And raises questions about the total lack of interest from the rest of the league.
