#2815. Gameday column: Bills and the current state of strength of schedule

It's like each week there's arisen a new narrative about this Buffalo Bills team.

Leading into Week 11 against the Dolphins, it was all about strength of schedule… probably because of Buffalo's competition — or lack thereof — since the bye week.

I'll be the first to mention that teams are not always what their record says they are.

But what these Bills are doing is subtly different from previous Bills teams during the Josh Allen era.

What has remained the same is Buffalo's regular-season dominance.

Since 2020, the Bills have been the NFL's best team in the regular season. They have the most wins and the highest point differential by an insane margin.

But like I wrote a week ago, the Sean McDermott clubs always had an uncharacteristic stumble or two during said regular season that kept them away out of the No. 1 seat in the AFC playoffs.

Of course today, against the Dolphins, represents one of those "stumble" possibilities with the colossal clash on Thanksgiving against the Chiefs looming. But that's the not the crux of this column.

Are the Bills the best 8-1 team in NFL history? No. Are they the best 8-1 team in the past decade. No.

But they've done exactly what a team should do against the competition level it has faced.

Regarding the aforementioned point differential, it's my absolute favorite measure of team quality… because it's straightforward and pretty darn predictive.

How's this for point differential's stickiness — 11 of the past 12 Super Bowl winners finished the regular season in the Top 6 in point differential. Insane, right? From 2004 – 2025, 17 of 22 of the clubs that hoisted the Lombardi Trophy at season's end were a Top 6 point-differential team during the regular season.

And this Bills team enters this AFC East game against the Dolphins second in point differential, at +88.

If Buffalo had been squeaking by lesser opponents regularly, I'd be the first to write their 8-1 feels phony.

And teams can only play the opponents on their schedule.

Among the nine teams at 7-2 or better right now, only the Broncos (.583) and Vikings (.518) have faced a cumulative opponent win percentage above .500. The Bills are third on that list at .480, as their opponents have a combined record of 40-47. The combined record of Chiefs' opponents to date is 34-53 (.391). They're currently in last in that metric.

(another shoutout to Jeremy White from WGR550 who noticed this earlier this week.)

Oh, and as for the strength-of-schedule element that legitimately matters come playoff-seeding time — the Bills currently have the second-best strength of victory at .455, behind only the Broncos, the sole AFC club with a strength of victory over .500.

On this note, the first half of the 2026 NFL season has shown a more sizable gap between the great teams and the noticeably bad ones.

Ahead of Week 11, nine teams with seven or more wins is the most in any season in the NFL since at least 2001. Eight clubs with seven or more losses is tied for the second-most in that time frame.

And, heck, the Bills are 16.5-point favorites at home in the division. The Chiefs are 15.5-point favorites at home against the Cardinals. The Broncos are 10.5-point favorites against the Raiders at home. And the Jaguars are 6-point favorites on the road against the 5-3-1 Giants. Vegas thinks the dichotomy will continue.

The Bills can't focus on proving themselves today against the Dolphins. And they don't have to be concerned about that. They need to stack another win to get to 9-1 before their lengthy test of the holiday season that fittingly begins on Thanksgiving and ends on Christmas, a five-game stretch against the Chiefs, Patriots, Packers, Bears, and Broncos.

We'll know who this Bills team is by then.

But entering that stretch with the AFC's best record possible needs to be the emphasis of today's goal. The quality of opponent is irrelevant.

#2814. Takeaways from UH's win at West Virginia: Cougars bounce back, Keisean Henderson arrives

MORGANTOWN, W.Va. - Five takeaways from No. 20 Houston's 34-31 victory over West Virginia on Saturday:

How would the Cougars respond after a disappointing performance in a 42-32 loss at Colorado?

The Cougars showed plenty of fight and, while the defense still showed some cracks and the offense sputtered at times, it was a Big 12 road win. UH also remains in position to reach the 10-win mark in consecutive seasons for the first time in program history.

"We had to flush it out and come back stronger," running back Makhi Hughes said. "That's what we did."

All the talk has been about the Keisean Henderson era beginning in 2027. Well, that's still the case, but UH fans got a sneak peek when the true freshman made his first career start in place of injured fifth-year senior Conner Weigman. Henderson had some impressive moments (his 68-yard touchdown pass to Amare Thomas on the third play of the game) but it was what he didn't do that should be praised.

The heralded freshman knew when to throw the ball away. He knew when not to try and do too much. He trusted his pass protection and had enough patience to stay in the pocket long enough to see things develop. He also showed his ability to run, including an 18-yard keeper.

His game management down the stretch resembled that of a veteran, not a first-year quarterback who had been limited to 17 snaps all season. The future is bright for Henderson.

You'll get get in-depth news and analysis about UH's basketball and football teams from reporter Joseph Duarte by signing up for the Cougar Confidential newsletter.

UH's offensive line, a Joe Moore Award semifinalist for the best in the nation, gave Keisean Henderson plenty of time to work. Henderson averaged 3.19 seconds to throw on all dropbacks, according to Pro Football Focus. Only one of Henderson's two sacks was credited to the offensive line. "We got a couple of blitzes and got a little pressure on him, but outside of that, it was way too much time to throw the ball," West Virginia coach Rich Rodriguez said. We'll give the line a pass for the three false starts.

That seems to be what the UH defense has seen in the past month. It was another heavy dose of runs Saturday as the Cougars allowed a season-high 329 yards to the Mountaineers. That followed the 249 yards allowed by UH in a double-digit loss at Colorado. In the first seven games this season, UH was among the best in the nation, allowing an average 82.6 yards per game. In the last four games, UH is allowing 241.5 points per game. But as one UH staffer said leaving the field, "We made the stops when it mattered."

On its final five drives, West Virginia missed a field goal and had four punts. After allowing a touchdown on the opening drive of the second half, UH did not allow any points over the final 27 ½ minutes.

Jonathan Dimas has been money all season, connecting on 13 of his 15 field goal attempts, an 86.7% success rate.

Dimas delivered a 23-yard field goal as time expired and had a 34-yarder late in the third quarter. His 54-yard try just before halftime had the distance but hit the left upright. It would have been a school record.

A week earlier, Dimas had a career-high 52-yard field goal at Colorado.

"Tremendous season," coach Willie Fritz said.

#2812. Down Tatum, White, and Robinson, Celtics lose to lowly Kings 97-96

The Celtics suffered their most embarrassing loss of the season on Saturday night, losing to the now two-win Sacramento Kings, 97-96.

It was the third quarter that pushed the Kings over the top as Boston shot 5-of-26 from the field (missed all thirteen three-pointers) in the period and were outscored 35-13. It was a great game for Mike Conley, who scored 19 points and was a +17.

The Celtics were missing three key players in this one; Jayson Tatum, Derrick White and Mitchell Robinson all sat out this game. They started Payton Pritchard, Sam Hauser, Paul George, Hugo Gonzalez and Neemias Queta. It was the first start of the season for both Hauser and Pritchard. Meanwhile, the Kings started, Darius Acuff Jr., Zach LaVine, Keegan Murray, De'Andre Hunter and Domantas Sabonis. Ben Simmons and Maxime Raynaud remained out as neither player has played all season.

It was a really hot start for the Celtics as they scored the first 7 points of the game — buckets from Pritchard, Queta and George, led to a Sacramento timeout.

The C's missed 11 of their first 13 three point attempts as Sacramento went on a 21-5 run to take a 23-19 lead.

Sam Hauser hit a corner three at the buzzer to give the Celtics a 27-25 lead at the end of the first quarter. Hauser led the team in scoring with 6 points while Hugo Gonzalez had 5.

Mike Conley was the first point guard off of the bench instead of Devin Carter in what was his return to Sacramento. Conley chipped in right away with 5 points and 2 steals in his first 4 minutes of action.

He then had a sweet pass to Ron Harper Jr.

The Celtics took a lot of threes in the first half but most of them did not go in. They went 9/33 from beyond the arc in the half. They also fouled too much in the first half; the Kings spent much of the 2nd quarter in the bonus.

The Celtics led 54-41 after the first half. Hauser had 4 of the 9 Celtics threes and 12 points. The defense was awesome holding the Kings to 41 points and being the reason Boston led by 13 at the break.

The Celtics three-point struggles continued into the 3rd quarter as the Kings made a run to cut the Celtics lead to 2 at 60-58. The primary reason for the Kings run was the Celtics defense fell off. The Kings scored 17 points in the first 6:31 of the 3rd quarter. The Celtics called a timeout and turned it over out of the timeout as Sacramento tied the game. The Kings led entering the 4th, 76-67.

There are really no words to describe how the quarter went. They were bad on offense and defense and this is against the worst 3rd quarter team in the NBA entering the night — the Kings 3rd quarter net rating had been -23.7.

The Celtics started the 4th quarter with a new lineup.

They scored the first 1o points of the quarter to take the lead by 1. Mike Conley had 6 of the 10 points.

The two teams were tied at 82 after the Kings regained a 5-point lead but another Conley three and a Luka Garza put back knotted the game up.

Then, on a night the Celtics could not buy a three, Neemias Queta hit a three.

Darius Acuff hit a tough mid-range shot to put the Kings up 1, followed by a Paul George offensive rebound to put the Celtics up 1, then Acuff made 2 free throws to put the Kings up 1 with 40.5 seconds to go.

After Queta and Acuff traded 2 free throws each, Payton Pritchard, who had struggled all night, hit a clutch mid-range shot to put the Celtics up 1 with 11.8 seconds to go as the Kings called timeout.

Keegan Murray got an offensive rebound and put back right over Devin Carter and Hugo Gonzalez to put the Kings up 97-96 with 5 seconds left.

Payton Pritchard missed what would have been a game winning shot and the Celtics lost 97-96 as the Kings got their 2nd win of the season.

In a game Boston lost by 1 points, they missed 10 free throws.

For what it's worth, Darius Acuff Jr. is a star. The way he took over the game late after struggling all night was really impressive.

The C's shot 34% from the field and 22% from three while the Kings shot 41% from the field and 27% from three. The Celtics next game is Sunday in San Francisco against the Warriors at 8:30 pm EST.

#2807. Mike Brown pushing for faster offense from sluggish Knicks

"Last-last," Brown asked, "or second-to-last?"

Entering Saturday, the Knicks sat at the very bottom of the NBA in pace, averaging 94.91 possessions per game. Brown repeatedly has urged his team to play with pace since taking over last year, but it's a rare desire that has not come to fruition.

New York Knicks head coach Mike Brown reacts during the fourth quarter against the Portland Trail Blazers in New York, Saturday, Nov. 21, 2026. Getty Images

The reality is that this is not an overly athletic roster that thrives in transition. This group of veteran players never will be a unit that loves to get up and down the floor. Jalen Brunson, Karl-Anthony Towns and OG Anunoby are more methodical scorers who prefer to set up themselves in the frontcourt.

Brown knows that, so while he will continue to preach pace, he values something else that serves as an indication of whether his offense is functioning properly, something that doesn't necessarily require the natural athleticism they lack.

"We have to make sure that every time we cut, it's a scoring cut and our decisions are quick," Brown said before a 97-96 Knicks win, which required a buzzer-beating 3 from Mikal Bridges to escape after blowing a 26-point second-half lead.

When the Knicks offense kicked into gear during their championship run last year, it was evident.

With Towns operating as a hub, the four other Knicks on the floor constantly cut and set off-ball screens around him. The ball moved quickly, swinging side to side constantly. Towns, along with Anunoby and Jordan Clarkson, did not play Saturday.

Landry Shamet, who scored 20 points, shoots a jumper over Toumani Camara during the Knicks' buzzer-beating win over the Trail Blazers on Nov. 21, 2026, at the Garden. Jason Szenes for New York Post

"You can tell when a guy goes from Point A to Point B with a little bit of velocity," Brown said. "A guy like Landry Shamet, he is doing it every single time. With him shooting the ball the way he's shooting it, that gravity that he carries when he moves, you can't really see it or feel it if you don't know the game well. But when he moves that quickly from Point A to Point B, everybody is just leaning just a little bit in that direction, which may create an opening or an advantage for your teammate that's away from him."

It's a reason why Shamet's role keeps growing. Among the five starters, though, it's something they've had to reestablish after a sluggish first few games of the season.

The Knicks aren't quick in transition. Once they're set in the frontcourt, though, they're capable of playing quickly.

It's when they are at their best. It takes pressure off Brunson having to orchestrate everything. It sets up open catch-and-shoot opportunities for Anunoby and Mikal Bridges. It allows Josh Hart to utilize his passing ability and set up teammates.

"Trying to get everybody to do that all the time is huge," Brown said. "Because the defense doesn't have a chance to catch up, they don't have a chance to get set, and they just gotta react. And sometimes they'll overreact because you're going so hard. And you can see it as the game goes along. If guys aren't doing it, we try to mention it to him."

A slow team finding a way to play fast — that's crucial to the Knicks' success.
